The council chamber smelled of lemon oil and old paper, the same smell that had met Wren every Tuesday since she was tall enough to see over the table. Twelve chairs curved around it in a half circle, and tonight eleven were taken. Denise's chair at the far end sat empty.

Wren stood at the presentation board with a wooden pointer in her hand. The map pinned behind her was her own work: the creek's switchback in blue ink, the orchard boundary in green, the store, the substation, and the archive building going up on the old Miller lot. She had redrawn it four times that week rather than think about what she was about to ask.

"Thank you for coming on short notice," she said. "I know harvest is eating everyone's evenings."

Yusuf shifted in the center chair, the new jacket Odette had made him buy pulling across his shoulders. "Harvest waits. This doesn't. Wren, the floor's yours."

She took the first page off her stack. A timeline, dense with dates.

"Corwin Drake has been quiet for twelve months. I don't think that means he's gone." She tapped the paper. "It's been nineteen years since Millbrook, his first attempt, and he failed because he only had part of the picture. He has spent the years collecting the rest. Property records, census gaps, newspaper archives from three counties, and a court filing from a 1923 land dispute that mentions 'the arrangement' without ever saying what it is."

Priya's pen stopped. "He has documentation."

"He has fragments, which is enough to be dangerous. He doesn't want to break the bargain. He wants to own its terms. If he publishes what he has, a partial history with the context stripped out and a conspiracy stapled on, the valley becomes a story the outside world can't ignore. Reporters. Researchers. People with cameras and questions and no idea what living here costs."

"Then we stop him," Odette said, arms folded. "We've stopped worse."

"Not with a ward or a working. He's using the truth, or enough of it to pass for the whole. The only answer to that is more truth, organized and sourced and complete." Wren turned the page to a table of contents. "I'm proposing the Archive Project. A full public record of the valley's history: the bargain's terms, the lineages, every renewal and every failure, the debts paid and the debts still owed. Published in our words, on our schedule, before Drake publishes his."

Nobody spoke. Walt Pruitt cleared his throat.

"Everything," he said. "The memory trades. The toll. The reason this valley never shows up right on a phone."

"Yes."

"And the people who paid." He looked at Priya, then at Odette, then at the empty chair. "Their names. What they lost."

"Redacted wherever living family objects. But yes. If the record isn't honest, it's just another weapon."

Yusuf didn't wait. "You have the council's backing and full access to the municipal records. The archive building is yours once the roof is watertight. Odette?"

"Two weeks," Odette said. "Three if the weather turns."

"Then we make it official in two. Wren leads. Priya, you're liaison for anything that touches an active investigation. Odette, Walt, whatever she needs from the store or the town, she gets it."

Wren let out a breath she had been holding since Sunday. "Thank you."

"Don't thank me yet." Yusuf said it gently. "You're proposing to put the valley's whole history in strangers' hands. Are you sure?"

"The alternative is letting someone else tell our story. We tried that. It cost Caleb two years of his memory and it cost Denise fifty years of keeping the secret. I won't do it again."

The meeting broke up twenty minutes later. Walt lingered over his coat.

"Talks just like her grandmother," he told Priya on his way out. "Never once asked whether we were comfortable."

Priya waited until the door shut. "You held the room."

"I had the facts."

"You had the facts and you didn't apologize for them. Most people apologize." She capped her pen. "What's bothering you?"

Wren squared her pages against the table. "If Drake gets to the state archives first. Or the county clerk, or the historical society."

"He won't." Priya's voice carried the low hum of a Caller, the calm that reached anyone within five feet. "Yusuf already called the county recorder and the Millbrook courthouse. They're expecting our requests."

"And if he has copies? Digital ones? He's had nineteen years."

"Then we publish louder and cleaner, with the whole valley behind us." The calm flickered for half a second, the bill for what she had just spent. "You aren't doing this alone, Wren. That's the point of a project."

Wren nodded. It helped less than it should have.

The Castellanos found things. That was the family trade, every generation back to the founding: lost rings, lost people, lost truths, and each finding cost the finder a memory of the search. Her grandmother had paid with the memory of finding her own husband in the creek. Her mother had paid with the night Wren's father left. Wren knew about her own small payments only from the gaps. A wedding ring in the orchard mud when she was fourteen, which its owner still thanked her for every Christmas. A missing case file she had located for Priya three years ago, remembered now only through the thank-you note in her desk drawer. She had never yet paid for anything large.

Her grandmother had drilled the family rule into her before she could read. *We find so others don't have to look.* The gift was a shield, and tonight Wren had asked the council to lower it and hand the world a map.

She locked the chamber and walked down Main Street toward the archive. The October air smelled of woodsmoke and windfall apples fermenting in the orchard grass. Light spilled from the windows of Odette's store, which never really closed. Odette claimed she would sleep on the counter if the town ever needed Band-Aids at midnight.

The archive stood three lots down, its roof still under blue tarps that snapped in the wind, scaffolding clinging to the east wall. Inside were bare studs and subfloor, the smell of cut lumber and drywall dust, and boxes stacked along every wall: municipal records, family donations, the valley's paper memory. One carton still wore the label *Pruitt Hardware, invoices, 1931* in Walt's father's handwriting, and another held nothing but church bulletins tied up with kitchen twine.

She set her pages on the folding table she had claimed as a desk. She would need a real one, and a chair that didn't wobble, and shelving, and a catalog system. She would need Theo Marsh, who was arriving in the morning.

She had written to the Millbrook County Records Office three weeks ago, before the council vote, because waiting for permission had already cost the valley enough. *We require a records investigator with archival training. No gift necessary. Discretion essential.* She had expected a refusal, or a referral, or silence. The answer had come on county stationery, one line in ink that bled a little into the paper: *I accept. Two weeks' notice. Credentials enclosed. T. Marsh.*

The credentials were still in her bag, photocopied and certified. Theo Marsh, twenty-six. A history degree from Millbrook State, three years at the county records office, two more in special collections at the state archives. A published paper on gaps in rural land records, 1890 to 1920. A recommendation from the state archivist calling him exacting to the point of obsession, and the only researcher she trusted with fragile originals.

Nothing about a gift. Nothing about Kindred blood. He was ordinary, entirely and on purpose, and he would be here in the morning.

Wren lowered herself onto the wobbling chair. Drake had looked, and he had found fragments, and he was fitting them together. The only defense was a record better than his, and she could not build it alone.

---

The knock came at 8:17 the next morning.

Wren had been in the archive since six with a magnifying lamp and a migraine gathering behind her left eye. The municipal ledgers were a mess: three different hands, two numbering systems, and a gap from 1918 to 1922 that looked like fire damage but left no scorch on the pages.

The man on the threshold was taller than she had pictured. Mid-twenties, though he held himself like someone older, hands in the pockets of a wool coat worn soft at the cuffs. Short dark hair. He had the stillness of a person who had spent years waiting in rooms where waiting was the job. He neither smiled nor offered a hand, and she found she didn't mind.

"Theo Marsh. County records. You wrote to us."

"Wren Castellano. Valley documentation project."

He came in slowly, reading the room: the boxes, the exposed studs, the single bulb on a temporary wire, the lamp and the ledger stacks and the notebook where she had been transcribing 1917. "Rough conditions."

"Temporary. The roof is tarped and shingles start in two weeks if it doesn't rain. Shelving next week. The coffee is instant, the water is from the tap, and there's a portable toilet behind the building until the septic is connected."

"I've worked in worse." He took a manila folder from his coat, its corners soft from handling. "Credentials, background check, and the county's confidentiality agreement for sensitive municipal work." From his bag came a second folder. "And a personal project, if there's downtime. Land records for the Millbrook area, 1880 to 1930. There's a hole in the index I've been chasing for six years."

Wren skimmed the agreement. Standard language. "The valley isn't a municipal contract. We're not incorporated. We don't have a charter."

"The county has treated you as a special district for record-keeping since 1873." He said it to the folder rather than to her. "The paperwork is thin, but it exists. Someone kept it up, quietly."

Her fingers stopped on the page. "Who?"

"It doesn't say. There's one notation: 'Per standing arrangement, Amity Falls records filed separately. Access restricted.' That's the hole. The files exist, fourteen linear feet of them, but the index gives no classification code, no retention schedule, no authorized staff. Nobody at the county knows what the arrangement is." He looked up at last. "I wrote a paper on it. The archivist said I was inventing a conspiracy where bureaucracy would do. Then your letter came with 'discretion essential' in it, and I thought, the hole has a name."

The lamp hummed. Outside, a truck downshifted on Main Street.

"You aren't Kindred," Wren said.

"No."

"No gift at all."

"None. I have patience and an eye for a bad index."

"You'll want to see everything. The renewal logs, the debt ledgers, the names."

"I'll read everything I'm given. What goes into the public version is a different argument, and I'd rather have it after I know what's there." He said it plainly, as if it were a shelf-mark. "Somebody else is building a record of this place, aren't they? Nobody asks for an archivist with discretion unless they're racing something."

She hadn't expected him to hear that in a line of stationery. "Someone is. We need to finish first."

"Then we should stop talking and start indexing."

Wren studied him. He was exactly what she had asked for. He was also a stranger with no blood in the bargain and no gift to teach him what its records had cost the people written in them. He would read every page, and he would want every page to be true. That was the job, and it frightened her a little anyway. *We find so others don't have to look.* But he had looked. He had found the hole without anyone's help, and he had not stopped.

"We start with the ledgers from 1917 forward," she said. "The gap years are in a separate box, and it's in bad shape. Fire, water, and a third kind of damage I haven't identified."

"Damaged pages are still pages."

He slid the county's agreement across the table. She signed *Wren Castellano* on the line for Project Lead. He signed *Theodore Marsh* under Contractor, Records Investigation, in a small square hand.

They worked through the day with very little talk. By noon her migraine had eased, and it took her a while to work out why. He had turned the lamp on its arm while she was in the back room, so the glare fell across the page instead of across her eyes, and he had not said a word about it. Odette sent her nephew over with two sandwiches in wax paper and a note that read *Feed the new one.* Theo ate his standing up, one hand still on a ledger, and asked what the town's fire records looked like for the winter of 1918. Wren told him there weren't any. He wrote that down as though it were an answer.

At six he put his coat back on and nodded at the door, and then he was gone. The archive felt larger without him, and quieter. The single bulb threw long shadows across the subfloor.

Wren stayed at the table with the signed agreement under her hand. She had been raised to keep the door shut, and this morning she had opened it to a man she had known for ten hours. She switched off the lamp.

On her way out she passed the fire-damaged box, which she had meant to save for tomorrow. He had already found it. The lid was off, the pages inside squared and interleaved with clean paper, and across the front ran a strip of masking tape lettered in small, neat pencil.

*1918 to 1922. Start here.*


---

The basement of the old county building smelled of dust and iron and the slow rot of paper that had been wet once and never properly dried. Wren had spent the better part of a year learning that smell. She knew which shelves sagged under the weight of water damage, which boxes mice had chewed through, which ledgers held ink faded to brown ghosting before she was born.

Theo stood at the long table under the single bare bulb, gloves already on, a magnifying lamp clamped to the edge. His own tools from Millbrook sat beside him: a bone folder, Japanese tissue patches, wheat starch paste in a small sealed jar. He worked like a surgeon warned the patient might wake mid-operation.

"The 1847 ledger," he said without looking up. "The binding is shot. The spine cracked when I lifted it. Turn the pages wrong and we lose the gutter margins."

Wren set her notebook down on a clear patch of table. "The 1847 ledger lists the first renewal after the original bargain. Names in the margin, amounts in the column beside them. If we photograph it now, we get the names before the ink flakes off completely."

"We photograph it after I stabilize the spine. Two hours. Maybe three."

"Drake doesn't wait two hours."

Theo's hand paused over the cover. The leather was red-rotted, powdering under his fingertips. "Drake has had his partial record for nineteen years. He waited through the ring ritual. He waited through Denise's release. He waited while you decided what to do with Adelaide's letter and Whitlock's letter. He is not going to publish tonight because we took three hours to save a binding."

"He might publish tomorrow because he sees we're stalling."

"He sees us being careful. There is a difference."

Wren crossed her arms. Cold seeped through her wool socks, the kind that rises from stone. She could feel the weight of the valley above them, orchards, creek, houses where people slept without knowing the full history of what kept them safe. She had grown up in that not-knowing, resented it. Now she was the one deciding how much of it to hand over, and the resentment had curdled into something heavier.

"The margin notes on page twelve," she said. "The ones in pencil. They're not in the index. They're not in any of Dev's transcriptions. If that page tears while you're mending the spine, we lose the only record of who paid the 1847 toll and what memory was taken."

Theo turned the magnifying lamp toward the page. The pencil was faint, pressure uneven, as if the writer's hand had shaken. He adjusted the light, frowning.

"Three names," he murmured. "Two amounts. One blank where the memory should be described. The handwriting changes halfway through the second entry."

"Which means two different recorders. Which means the blank might not be an omission. It might be a refusal to write it down."

"Or the recorder didn't know what memory was taken. The Finder who paid the toll might not have told them."

"Finders always know. The cost comes due at the moment of finding. You feel the gap open. You feel what slides into it."

Theo looked up. His face was pale in the lamp light, shadows sharp under his cheekbones. Three weeks in the valley. He had slept above Odette's store, eaten breakfast at Mara and Caleb's table more mornings than not. Not once had he asked to see the bargain site, or meet a Warden, a Mender, or a Weather. He had asked for the ledgers, the correspondence, the council minutes, the index with its restricted gap. He had asked for the paper trail.

"You've paid a debt," he said. "A small one. You said a note in a drawer, a thank-you that puzzled you."

Wren's throat tightened. "A grocery list. My grandmother's handwriting. I found it in the kitchen drawer three days after she died. Apples, flour, tea. She had written it the morning she helped Mrs. Kowalski locate her wedding ring down the well. The thank-you note was from Mrs. Kowalski, dated the same day. My grandmother never mentioned the ring. Never mentioned the cost. The grocery list was the only thing missing from her memory that week."

"And you knew because..."

"Because I found the list. Because I'm a Finder. Because the gap showed me the shape of what was gone before I even understood what a gap was."

Theo set down the bone folder. He removed his gloves slowly, finger by finger, and laid them flat on the table.

"That's a small debt," he said. "You've never paid a large one."

"No."

"And you've never redirected a toll."

"No. My grandmother taught me that redirecting is the oldest taboo. She taught me that before she taught me how to hold a finding rod."

"Dev redirected a toll. He paid Caleb's debt with someone else's memory. He served a sentence for it. Now he does evidence work for the substation."

"I know Dev's history. I was there for the ring ritual. I held one of the stones."

Theo was quiet. The basement settled around them, old timbers creaking, a drop of water hitting stone somewhere in the dark behind the shelves.

"The blank on page twelve," he said. "If it's a redirected toll, the recorder might not have known whose memory was taken. The Finder would know. The person who lost the memory wouldn't."

"Which means the gap in the record isn't an accident. It's a concealment."

"Or it's a gap the recorder couldn't fill. There's a difference between hiding something and not having the information to write down."

Wren moved to the table, leaning over the page. Pencil marks smudged, paper thin as onion skin. Pressure marks showed where the writer had pressed hard, then lighter, then hard again.

"The second name," she said. "The one with the blank. The handwriting changes right after it. As if the first recorder stopped and someone else took over."

"Or the same recorder changed grip. Changed pen. Changed posture."

"Finders don't change posture when they're recording a toll. The cost hits at the moment of finding. The recording happens after. The hand that writes the name is the hand that paid."

Theo reached for the magnifying loupe on its chain around his neck. He bent over the page, breath slow and controlled.

"The first name," he said. "Ambrose Whitlock. The amount: seven years. The memory: his sister's face. The second name... the handwriting is different. The pressure is different. The name is..."

He stopped. The loupe hovered.

"The name is what?"

"Castellan. Not Castellano. Castellan. One 't'. Two 'l's. One 'n'."

Wren went cold. The basement seemed to contract, walls pressing inward. Castellan. The ring member from the earlier trouble. The one coerced by Ambrose Kell. The one who had served on the ring that distributed Dev's taking.

"That's not my family," she said. "My grandmother was Castellano. Two 't's. Two 'l's. One 'n'. My father was Castellano. I'm Castellano."

"I know. I checked the valley records when I arrived. Your line goes back to the 1820s. The Castellan line appears in the 1860s. Different families. Different gifts. The Castellans were Weathers."

"Then why is a Weather named Castellan paying a Finder's toll in 1847? Weathers don't pay Finder tolls. Weathers pay Weather tolls. The bargain tracks lineage."

"Unless the recorder made an error. Unless the name was misheard. Unless..."

"Unless the Castellan line married into the Castellano line and the gift shifted. Unless the recorder wrote what they were told and the teller lied."

Theo straightened, the loupe swinging against his chest. "You're jumping. One ambiguous entry in a damaged ledger. No corroborating record. No second source. A name that looks like yours but isn't, attached to a toll that doesn't match the lineage."

"Drake has this ledger. Or a copy. Or the index entry that points to it. That's why the index has a restricted gap. That's why the standing arrangement notation exists. Someone knew this entry was dangerous. Someone restricted access to it nineteen years ago, when Drake made his first attempt in Millbrook."

"Nineteen years ago the restricted notation was added. That doesn't mean Drake saw this page. It means someone in the county records office flagged it."

"Who? The county records office doesn't know about the bargain. They don't know about Finders or Weathers or tolls. They see ledgers with names and dates and amounts. A gap in the index gets flagged as restricted because they don't understand it."

"Then who flagged it?"

Wren stared at the pencil marks. The blank where the memory should be. The name that wasn't hers but looked like it could have been.

"Ambrose Whitlock," she said. "He held the Warden of the first debt role. He tried to end the bargain. He hid the complete terms. He taught Adelaide how to renew it. He left a letter warning his family not to share what he knew. His family buried the letter. Dev is his descendant. Dev found the letter."

"And Castellan?"

"Was on the ring. The ring that distributed Dev's taking. The ring that freed Denise. The ring that restored Caleb's memory. Ten volunteers. Castellan was one of them."

Theo's expression shifted, the dry professional mask cracking into something sharper. "You think Castellan knew about this entry. You think Castellan knew about a redirected toll in 1847, paid by a Weather with a name like yours, recorded by Ambrose Whitlock."

"I think the ring ritual didn't just distribute Dev's taking. I think it distributed knowledge. The volunteers held the stones. They felt the weight. They saw what Dev saw. Castellan might have seen this ledger in that moment. Might have known what it meant."

"And if Castellan knew, Drake might know. If Drake knows, he has leverage on the Castellan line. On the Castellano line by confusion. On the valley's history of redirected tolls."

"Drake doesn't want to destroy the valley. He wants control of the bargain's administrative terms. He wants the true original terms. A redirected toll in 1847, hidden by Whitlock, concealed in the index for nineteen years... that's administrative terms. That's the shape of the bargain's corruption."

Theo was silent. He replaced his gloves, snapped them at the wrists, picked up the bone folder again.

"Three hours to stabilize the spine," he said. "Then we photograph every page. Then we transcribe the margin notes. Then we cross-reference with the council minutes, the correspondence, the index. Then we see if there's a pattern."

"And if there is?"

"Then we publish the pattern. All of it. Before Drake publishes his version."

Wren nodded, though her chest was tight. She had come down thinking they were building a shield. Now she wondered if they were just mapping the target.

The stairs creaked. Wren turned, hand instinctively moving to the finding rod at her belt. Theo didn't startle. He had learned the valley's rhythms, knew the difference between a settler's step and a stranger's.

Yusuf appeared at the bottom of the stairs, his frame filling the narrow stairwell. The council chair moved with the deliberate care of a man who had once hesitated and learned the cost of hesitation. He carried a thermos in one hand, a folded paper in the other.

"Coffee," he said. "And a message from the county clerk's office. Delivered by a deputy who looked like she'd seen a ghost."

Wren took the thermos. The coffee was hot, bitter, exactly what she needed. "Priya?"

"Priya's at the substation with Dev. This was a different deputy. A temporary transfer from Millbrook. She didn't give her name. Handed this to the receptionist and left without signing the log."

Yusuf unfolded the paper. Single sheet, county letterhead, text crisp and official.

"Notice of Records Audit," Wren read over his shoulder. "Pursuant to County Ordinance 44-12, the Millbrook County Records Division will conduct a comprehensive audit of all restricted-index holdings in the Amity Falls municipal archive, commencing fourteen days from date of service. All materials flagged with standing-arrangement notation are subject to review, duplication, and potential transfer to the county central repository."

The paper trembled in her fingers. Fourteen days. The county central repository. Duplication.

"Theo," she said. "The restricted gap in the index. The standing arrangement notation. This is what you came for. This is the professional reason."

Theo took the paper, read it once, read it again. His face went flat, the way it did when he was thinking through procedures, jurisdictions, chains of custody.

"The audit was scheduled," he said. "Before I arrived. The notice was prepared weeks ago. The deputy who delivered it, a temporary transfer from Millbrook. Drake has contacts in the Millbrook records office. He had them nineteen years ago. He has them now."

"Can we stop it?" Yusuf asked. "The council can petition. Claim local jurisdiction. Argue the materials are culturally sensitive, historically fragile, not subject to county ordinance."

"Petitions take months," Theo said. "The audit starts in fourteen days. The county central repository is in Millbrook. Drake's territory. Once the materials leave this building, we lose control of the chain of custody. He doesn't need to publish his partial record. He just needs to be in the room when the county makes copies."

Wren set the thermos down hard. Coffee sloshed over the rim, dark on the concrete floor.

"We have fourteen days," she said. "To photograph, transcribe, cross-reference, and publish the complete true history. Before the county audit hands Drake the leverage he's been building for nineteen years."

"Fourteen days," Theo repeated. "For the 1847 ledger alone: three hours for the spine, two for photography, four for transcription. Cross-referencing the council minutes, forty years of them. The correspondence boxes, Dev said seventeen. The index itself needs a full audit to find every restricted notation."

"We don't do it alone," Yusuf said. "Priya can compel truth from the deputies handling the transfer. Dev can map substation evidence to ledger entries. Mara can coordinate volunteers. Caleb handles logistics. Odette manages the store as a drop point. Denise knows the bargain's terms better than anyone alive. Walt can coordinate the town."

"Denise is seventy-eight," Wren said. "She only just started aging naturally. She shouldn't be carrying this."

"Denise carried the bargain for fifty years in secret," Yusuf said. "The weight of every renewal, every toll, every memory taken. She carried it alone. She doesn't need your permission to carry it now."

Wren looked at the ledger. The pencil marks. The blank where the memory should be. That name again, the one that could have been hers.

"What about my mother?" she asked. "She lives in the valley. She's alive. She doesn't know the full history. She knows I'm a Finder, that I do documentation work. She doesn't know about the bargain's terms. She doesn't know about the tolls."

"Your mother," Yusuf said gently, "raised a daughter who became the valley's lead documenter at twenty-one. A daughter who held a ring stone and felt the weight of ten volunteers sharing a debt. A daughter about to publish the truth the valley has hidden for generations. Whatever she knows, whatever she doesn't... she raised you. Trust that."

Wren's eyes stung. She blinked hard, looking down at the ledger.

"Three hours for the spine," she said. "Then we start."

Theo adjusted the magnifying lamp. He picked up the wheat starch paste jar, unscrewed the lid. The paste smelled of starch and time.

"One page at a time," he said. "One ledger at a time. One name at a time."

Yusuf set the audit notice on the table, weighted it with a paperweight from the shelf. "I'll tell the council. We'll mobilize. Fourteen days. We've done impossible things in less time."

He climbed the stairs, footsteps heavy and sure. The basement door closed above them, cutting off the sound of the valley waking.

Wren pulled on her gloves. She picked up her notebook, opened to a fresh page. The date went down first. Then: 1847 ledger. Ambrose Whitlock, seven years, sister's face. Castellan (Weather?), amount illegible, memory blank. Handwriting change. Possible redirected toll. Index restriction added 19 years ago. County audit in 14 days.

"Your grandmother's grocery list," Theo said quietly. "The thank-you note. You said the list was the only thing missing from her memory that week. How did you know it was missing? If the gap took the memory of writing the list, how did you know the list existed to be missing?"

Wren's pen stopped. Ink pooled at the tip.

"Because I found the list in the drawer," she said. "Because I'm a Finder. Because the gap showed me the shape of what was gone. The list was there, in the drawer, in her handwriting. But when I held it, I didn't remember her writing it. Didn't remember her holding the pen. Didn't remember the morning light on the kitchen table. The memory of writing it was gone. The list itself remained. The object remained. The memory of its making was the cost."

"And the thank-you note?"

"Mrs. Kowalski brought it three days later. My grandmother read it. She smiled. Said, 'How lovely.' Didn't remember the ring. Didn't remember the well. Didn't remember the cost. Remembered the tea on the list. Remembered the apples. Remembered the flour. The gap was precise. Surgical. It took only the memory of the finding and the cost. It left the rest."

Theo was quiet. He applied paste to a strip of Japanese tissue with the bone folder, movements precise, unhurried.

"Precise," he said. "Surgical. That's how the bargain works. It takes exactly what's owed. No more, no less."

"Unless someone redirects the toll. Then it takes from someone who doesn't owe. Someone who didn't ask. Someone who doesn't know."

"Like Harriet Marsh. My grandmother. Ten years of her memory taken in Drake's first attempt. She doesn't know where she was. Doesn't know what she lost. Only knows there's a hole in her life shaped like a decade."

Wren looked at him. Really looked. The dry archivist. The patient researcher. The grandson who had built his adult life around a hole in his grandmother's mind.

"She's alive," Wren said. "You said she's alive. She's in Millbrook."

"In a care facility. Her childhood comes back. Her marriage. My mother's birth. Nothing from 1997 to 2007. The decade before 2007. The decade of Drake's first attempt. She cannot say where she was, what she did, who she was with. The memories aren't suppressed. They're gone. The bargain took them. Or Drake took them using the bargain's mechanism. We don't know which. We may never know."

"Does she know you're here?"

"She knows I work in records. Knows I travel. Asks me to bring the newspaper. Asks about the weather. Doesn't ask about the gap. Learned not to ask a long time ago. The doctors called it dissociative amnesia. The family called it a tragedy. I called it a clue."

"And now?"

"Now I have a ledger with a redirected toll from 1847. A county audit in fourteen days. An information broker who has waited nineteen years. A partner who feels the shape of every gap. And a grandmother who drinks tea and reads the newspaper and doesn't remember the decade that broke her."

Theo laid the tissue strip along the cracked spine. He smoothed it with the bone folder, slow strokes, firm pressure. The red rot powdered around the edges. The leather held.

"Three hours," he said. "Then we photograph. Then we transcribe. Then we cross-reference. Then we publish."

Wren watched his hands. The care. The patience. The knowledge that some things couldn't be rushed, that some repairs required time the world might not give.

"Fourteen days," she said. "We'll need coffee."

"I brought a case. It's in the trunk of the rental."

"Good. Because I have a feeling we're going to need more than coffee."

She picked up her finding rod. The weight was familiar, the wood worn smooth by her grandmother's hands before hers. She closed her eyes, let the gift extend, let it search the basement's dark corners, the shelves and boxes and ledgers. She felt the gaps. Small ones. A missing receipt. A forgotten conversation. A lost button. The ordinary losses of a building that had held records for a century.

And beneath them, deeper, older, a gap shaped like a name: Castellan. A gap shaped like a memory: a sister's face. A gap shaped like a decade, 1997 to 2007.

The rod warmed in her hand. The finding wasn't a pull. It was a knowing. The knowledge that something existed, somewhere, waiting to be located.

"Ready," she said.

Theo didn't look up from the spine. "Page twelve first. Then the rest. One at a time."

The basement settled around them. The bulb hummed. Somewhere above, the valley woke, ordinary and unaware, the orchards breathing in the morning light, the creek running clear, the houses full of people who didn't know the weight of the ground beneath their feet.

Wren opened the ledger to page twelve. The pencil marks waited. The blank waited. The name that wasn't hers stared up from the page, written in a hand that had shaken, recorded by a man who had tried to end the world and failed, hidden by a family that had buried the truth instead of sharing it.

Fourteen days.

She picked up her pen.


---

Morning light slanted through the high windows of the records room, catching dust in the air. Wren had been working since before dawn, the focus that came when the valley's history settled into her fingers like something she could hold. The folio boxes ran chronologically along the long table, each labeled in Yusuf's careful hand. She had catalogued three hundred and twelve in the past six weeks. Three hundred and thirteen, now, counting the one she had just opened.

The missing folio should have sat between the 1892 renewal and the 1897 renewal. A simple gap in the sequence. Five years of bargain history recorded, witnessed, and filed, according to the index Theo had built from the county records. The index said the folio existed. The shelf said it did not.

Wren set down the 1897 volume and pulled the 1892 volume closer. She turned the last page, then the inside back cover, then the back cover itself. Nothing tucked in. Nothing written in the margins. The binding was intact. The paper had the same texture as the others, the same faint watermark of the mill that had supplied the valley for generations.

She stood and walked the length of the table. Counted boxes. Counted again. The gap persisted.

"Missing," she said aloud. The word fell flat in the quiet room.

She had expected gaps. The valley's record was a living thing, and living things had holes. But this gap had a shape. Five years. Five renewals. The bargain renewed every year without fail, each one witnessed by the Warden of the first debt and recorded by whichever Finder held the duty at the time. Five consecutive years did not simply vanish from a locked room in a building that had never burned, never flooded, never been broken into.

She went to the door and opened it. The hallway stretched empty. The Archive building - still new enough that the pine floors smelled of resin and oil - held only her and the dust motes. Theo was at the county seat, chasing a lead in the Millbrook records that had gone cold nineteen years ago. Priya was on shift. Dev was at the substation with his evidence logs. The valley moved on around the work she had chosen.

Wren returned to the table. She sat. She opened the 1887 volume, the last one before the gap. The handwriting was precise, the ink dark. *Renewal witnessed. Terms upheld. Debt collected. Memory surrendered: the taste of honey on a summer morning, the sound of her mother's lullaby, the color of the sky the day her brother left.* The Finder's name was signed at the bottom: *Elena Castellano, Finder.*

Her grandmother. The name pulled at a familiar ache behind Wren's ribs.

She turned the page. *Renewal witnessed. Terms upheld. Debt collected. Memory surrendered: the scent of pine after rain, the feel of her father's hand on her shoulder, the word he spoke when he promised to return.* Same hand. Same signature. The entries continued, each one a small violence recorded in neat script. The bargain took what it was owed. The Finders wrote it down.

Wren knew this. She had known it since she was old enough to read the folios her grandmother left on the kitchen table. She had read them with the same casual attention she gave to the labels on preserve jars, the same way she learned which berries stained fingers purple and which ones caused rashes. The bargain was the valley. The valley was the bargain. The cost was the price of safety.

But she had never seen her grandmother write an entry. She had never watched the memory leave someone's face. She had been sent to the orchard, to the creek, to the upper meadow with a sketchbook and instructions to draw what she saw. *Go on now, Wren. This is grown work. You'll understand when you're older.*

She had been twelve when her grandmother died. Twelve, and the folios had passed to her mother, who had locked them in the cedar chest in the bedroom and never spoke of them again. *Not for you, Wren. Not yet. You don't need to carry this.*

Wren's fingers traced the edge of the 1892 volume. The paper was cool. The ink had faded to brown at the edges.

She had been eighteen when she found the first folio on her own. Not in the chest - her mother still held the key - but in the false bottom of her grandmother's writing desk, the one Wren had inherited. A single loose page, folded four times. *Renewal witnessed. Terms upheld. Debt collected. Memory surrendered: the memory of holding her granddaughter for the first time, the memory of her granddaughter's first steps, the memory of her granddaughter's name.*

Wren had stared at the page until the words blurred. She had refolded it. She had put it back. She had not told her mother. She had not asked what it meant to lose a name and still know the person who held it.

Now, at twenty-one, she sat in the Archive with the valley's history spread before her, and she understood that the missing folio was not an accident. Five years. Five renewals. Someone had removed them with the same care her grandmother used to record them.

The gap echoed.

Caleb's two years lost. The two years that had lived in a box on Ambrose Kell's shelf until Dev's evidence work found them. The way Caleb had described the absence - not a darkness, but a wrongness. *Like reaching for a tool you know should be in your pocket. Your fingers close on air. You don't know what's missing until you need it.*

Five years of bargain renewals. Five years of memories taken and recorded. Five years of the valley's debt paid in the currency of its people's lives.

Who had taken the folio? When? The Archive building had only been finished last autumn. Before that, the folios had lived in the council chamber's locked cabinet. Before that, in the Warden's house. The chain of custody was long, and every link had been a person the valley trusted.

Wren stood again. She needed Theo. She needed his county index, his methodical mind, his ability to see patterns in bureaucracy that she missed. She needed to know if the Millbrook records showed a corresponding gap, if Corwin Drake's partial history mentioned 1887 through 1892, if the standing arrangement notation in the county index had a date range that matched.

She needed to know if the missing folio was a valley secret or a Drake weapon.

She went to the window. The valley spread below, ordinary in the morning light. The orchard rows marched down the hillside. The creek caught the sun. Smoke rose from the general store's chimney. Odette would be opening soon, setting out the day's bread, turning the sign to *Open*. Walt Pruitt would be on his porch with his coffee, watching the road. Yusuf would be at the council table, reviewing the publication timeline Wren had given him yesterday.

Everything looked normal. Everything looked like the valley she had grown up in, the valley she had been protected from.

The protection had been a lie. Not a cruel one - her mother had loved her, her grandmother had loved her, the valley had wrapped around her like a blanket. But the blanket had been made of silence. *You don't need to carry this. Not yet. When you're older.*

She was older now. She was the Finder. The folios were hers to guard, hers to publish, hers to answer for.

The missing folio was a message. She did not know the sender. She did not know the recipient. But she knew the language. It was the language of the bargain: *something given, something taken, something recorded.*

She returned to the table. She pulled a fresh sheet of paper from the supply drawer. She wrote: *Gap identified: Folios 1887-1892 missing from Archive sequence. Index confirms existence. Physical volumes absent. Date of removal unknown. Chain of custody under review.*

She signed it: *Wren Castellano, Finder.*

Then she wrote a second line, slower: *Memory associated with gap: unknown. Cost of retrieval: unknown.*

She set the pen down. The ink dried in the slanted light.

The door opened behind her. She did not turn. The footsteps were light, deliberate. Priya's boots on the pine floor.

"Wren." Priya's voice was calm. The Caller gift hummed beneath it, a low current that made the air still. "Yusuf sent me. The publication schedule needs your sign-off on the 1890 through 1895 entries."

Wren turned. Priya stood in the doorway, her deputy badge catching the light. Her face was composed, but her eyes flicked to the open folios on the table, to the gap where a box should have been.

"Five years," Wren said. "1887 to 1892. The folios are gone."

Priya entered the room. She closed the door behind her. She walked to the table and stood beside Wren, looking down at the empty space.

"The county index has them," Priya said. "Theo sent the cross-reference last week. Three entries in the Millbrook ledger for those years. Standing arrangement notation on all three."

"Standing arrangement." Wren repeated the phrase. "The same notation that brought Theo here."

"Same notation." Priya's fingers rested on the edge of the 1892 volume. "Drake's partial history covers 1890 forward. He has the 1890 renewal. He has the 1891 renewal. He does not have 1887, 1888, or 1889."

"Because they're missing from here too."

"Or because he hasn't published them yet."

Wren looked at Priya. The Caller gift made lying difficult within five feet, but Wren was not asking for truth. She was asking for instinct.

"Which do you think?"

Priya was silent for a long moment. The gift settled around them, a pressure like the air before a storm.

"I think," Priya said, "that someone took those folios a long time ago. Before the Archive. Before the council chamber. Before the Warden's house burned in 1923. I think they've been missing for generations, and we only noticed because Theo built an index that told us what should be there."

"And the standing arrangement?"

"I think the standing arrangement is why they were taken. Or why they were hidden. The notation appears in the county ledger every year the valley renews. But those three years - 1887, 1888, 1889 - the notation is different. It says *standing arrangement suspended*."

Wren went cold. "Suspended."

"For three years. Then 1890: *standing arrangement resumed*. 1891: *standing arrangement resumed*. 1892: folio missing. 1893: folio present. Standing arrangement normal."

The words arranged themselves in Wren's mind. A pattern. A disruption. A return to normal.

"Three years suspended," Wren said. "Three folios missing before the five-year gap. 1887, 1888, 1889. Then 1890 and 1891 present in Drake's record. Then 1892 missing from here."

"Drake has 1890 and 1891," Priya corrected. "He doesn't have 1892. He has 1893 forward. The five-year gap in our Archive is 1887 through 1891. The folio for 1892 is the first one after the gap that should be here but isn't."

Wren counted on her fingers. "So the missing folios from our Archive are 1887, 1888, 1889, 1890, 1891. Five years. But Drake has 1890 and 1891. Which means the folios for those two years exist somewhere. They were taken from here but not destroyed. The other three - 1887, 1888, 1889 - might be destroyed. Or hidden elsewhere."

"Or never recorded," Priya said. "If the standing arrangement was suspended, maybe the bargain wasn't renewed those years. Maybe the debt wasn't collected. Maybe the memories weren't taken."

Wren shook her head. "The bargain renews every year. It has for two hundred years. The protection fails if it doesn't. Crops fail. Floods come. Sickness spreads. The valley would remember three years without protection."

"Would it?" Priya's voice was quiet. "The valley forgot Caleb's two years. The valley forgot Denise's fifty years. The valley forgot Ambrose Whitlock's attempt to end the bargain. We forget what we need to forget."

Wren looked at the 1887 folio. *Memory surrendered: the taste of honey on a summer morning, the sound of her mother's lullaby, the color of the sky the day her brother left.*

Her grandmother had written that. Her grandmother had witnessed the taking. Her grandmother had known the cost.

"Who was Warden of the first debt in 1887?" Wren asked.

"Ambrose Whitlock," Priya said. "He held the role from 1872 to 1901. The letter Adelaide found - his letter - said he tried to end the bargain in 1889. He failed. He hid the complete terms."

"1889," Wren said. "The last year of the suspension. The year he tried to end it."

"And the year before the folios reappear in Drake's record."

Wren's mind raced. Ambrose Whitlock. The Warden who tried to end the bargain. The ancestor Dev carried in his blood. The man who had hidden the complete terms rather than destroy them.

If the standing arrangement was suspended for three years - if the bargain was not renewed, or was renewed differently, or was challenged - then the missing folios were not missing by accident. They were evidence. Evidence of what happened when the Warden of the first debt tried to break the bargain and failed.

And someone had hidden that evidence. Someone had kept it from the valley for over a century.

"Drake has 1890 and 1891," Wren said slowly. "The years after the attempt. He doesn't have the attempt years. He doesn't have 1892. He has 1893 forward."

"His partial history is damning," Priya said. "But it's incomplete. He's missing the critical years. The years that would show what Whitlock did, what the bargain did, what the valley paid."

"Or what the valley didn't pay," Wren said. "If the arrangement was suspended, maybe the debt wasn't collected. Maybe the memories were returned. Maybe - "

She stopped. The thought was too large. Too dangerous.

"Maybe what?" Priya asked.

Wren looked at her friend. Priya's face was open, waiting. The Caller gift held the space between them, a circle of calm where truth could be spoken.

"Maybe the memories taken in 1890 and 1891 were different," Wren said. "Maybe they weren't the valley's memories. Maybe they were someone else's."

The words hung in the air. The prohibition was absolute. *Redirecting the toll* - paying your debt with someone else's memory - was the valley's oldest taboo. Dev had done it under coercion. He had paid for it with his freedom, with his sentence, with the weight of the ring ritual that had distributed the taking across ten volunteers.

But Dev was a Whitlock descendant. The bargain-ending practice ran in his blood. The Warden of the first debt had been a Whitlock.

If Ambrose Whitlock had tried to end the bargain in 1889, and failed, and the standing arrangement was suspended for three years...

"Who was Finder in 1890?" Wren asked.

Priya frowned. "The folios are signed. Elena Castellano was Finder until 1895. Your great-great-grandmother. She signed every renewal from 1870 to 1895."

Wren's grandmother's grandmother. The line stretched back, Finder to Finder, each one recording the cost.

"Elena Castellano," Wren repeated. "She would have known. She would have written it down."

"She wrote what she witnessed," Priya said. "If the debt was redirected, she would have recorded the memory taken. The folio would show whose memory was surrendered."

"Unless the folio was removed before it could be filed."

"Or unless the memory was taken from the Finder herself."

The silence stretched. The dust motes danced in the slanted light.

Wren thought of the loose page in the false bottom of the desk. *Memory surrendered: the memory of holding her granddaughter for the first time, the memory of her granddaughter's first steps, the memory of her granddaughter's name.*

Her grandmother had lost those memories. Her grandmother had paid the debt with her own memories of Wren.

But the folio said *memory surrendered* - not *memory redirected*. The language was precise. The Finders used precise language. *Surrendered* meant given freely, given from the self. *Redirected* meant taken from another.

Unless the language had been changed. Unless the folio had been rewritten. Unless the missing folios contained the truth that the surviving folios concealed.

"We need to find them," Wren said. "The missing folios. All of them. 1887 through 1892. If Drake has 1890 and 1891, he has two pieces of the puzzle. If we find the other three, we have the rest. We publish the complete record before he publishes his partial one."

"Before he publishes," Priya agreed. "Yusuf wants the first volume at the printer by month's end. The complete history. Adelaide's letter. Whitlock's letter. The ring ritual record. Everything."

"Everything except the missing folios."

"Everything except the missing folios."

Wren looked at the gap on the table. Five empty spaces in a row of boxes. Five years of the valley's life, removed from the record.

"Where do we look?" she asked.

Priya considered. "The Warden's house burned in 1923. The council chamber cabinet was moved to the Archive last autumn. The folios were inventoried at each transfer. The 1887 through 1891 folios were not in the inventory for the council chamber move. They were not in the inventory for the Archive move. They were missing before 1923."

"Before the fire."

"Before the fire. The last inventory that included them was 1910. The Warden's house inventory. After that, the records are... unclear."

"Unclear."

"The 1910 inventory was signed by the Warden of the first debt at the time. Ambrose Whitlock's successor. The role passed outside the bloodline after Whitlock stepped down in 1901. The next Warden was a Mender named Sarah Voss. Mara's great-grandmother."

"Mara's family."

"Mara's family. The Voss line held the Warden role from 1901 to 1947. Then it passed to a Weathers line. Then to a Caller line. Then, after a long vacancy, to Yusuf."

"So Yusuf is the current Warden."

"Only since Denise was freed, effectively. He was appointed Warden after the ritual that freed her. The role had been vacant since the previous Warden died in 1987."

Wren processed this. The Warden of the first debt - a role, not a bloodline. Held by Whitlock, then Voss, then Weathers, then Caller, then Yusuf. Each transition recorded. Each transition witnessed.

But the folios from 1887 to 1891 had vanished during Whitlock's tenure. Before the fire. Before the role passed to Sarah Voss.

"Who had access to the Warden's house in 1889?" Wren asked. "When Whitlock tried to end the bargain."

"His household," Priya said. "His staff. The Finder on duty - Elena Castellano. The Mender on duty. The Caller on duty. The valley's leaders. It would have been a small circle. The attempt was secret. Adelaide's letter says Whitlock acted alone, but he would have needed witnesses to the failure."

"Witnesses who knew the bargain could be challenged."

"Witnesses who knew the bargain could fail."

Wren's hand found the 1887 folio again. *Elena Castellano, Finder.*

Her great-great-grandmother. The woman who had recorded the cost for twenty-five years. The woman who had watched the bargain take memory after memory, year after year.

What had Elena seen in 1889? What had she written in the folios that were now missing?

"Dev," Wren said. "Dev is a Whitlock descendant. He has the bargain-ending practice in his blood. If anyone knows what Whitlock did, where the folios might be hidden..."

"Dev is at the substation," Priya said. "He's been there for three days. The evidence logs from the Kell case - he's cross-referencing them with the Drake material Theo sent. He said something about a pattern in the toll redirection records."

"Toll redirection records."

"The valley's oldest taboo. Dev found references in the substation archives. Cases going back two hundred years. Finders who redirected the toll. Finders who were caught. Finders who disappeared. He's building a timeline."

Wren felt a chill. "Dev is looking for redirected tolls. In the substation archives. While we have a missing folio that might prove the bargain was suspended and the debt was redirected."

"Dev doesn't know about the missing folio yet."

"He will. When Theo gets back. When we compare notes. When the publication deadline forces us to show our hand."

Priya was silent. The Caller gift held the room in its quiet pressure.

"Wren," she said. "If the missing folios show redirected tolls - if Whitlock's attempt to end the bargain involved redirecting the toll - then the valley's protection for those three years came from somewhere else. Someone else's memories. Someone else's life."

"I know."

"And if Elena Castellano recorded it... if she witnessed it... then she knew. And she kept the secret. And she passed the duty to her daughter, who passed it to her daughter, who passed it to your mother, who passed it to you. A hundred and thirty years of Finders keeping the same secret."

Wren's throat tightened. "My mother locked the folios in a cedar chest. She never let me read them. She told me I didn't need to carry it. Not yet. When I'm older."

"She was protecting you."

"She was protecting the secret. She was protecting the valley. She was protecting the bargain."

"She was protecting you," Priya repeated. "You were a child. You are her daughter. She loved you."

"She lied to me. By omission. By silence. By locking the truth in a chest and telling me it wasn't for me."

"She gave you the choice to open the chest when you were ready. You opened it. You became the Finder. You built the Archive. You brought Theo here. You're publishing the history. You're doing what she couldn't - what none of them could. You're telling the truth."

Wren looked at the gap on the table. The missing folios. The silence of five years.

"The truth," she said. "The complete truth. Not the partial truth Drake has. Not the hidden truth Whitlock buried. Not the protected truth my mother kept. All of it."

"All of it," Priya agreed. "And the cost?"

Caleb's two years lost, then found and returned. The ring ritual, ten volunteers sharing the weight of a single taking. Denise, freed after fifty years, aging naturally now, her face lining with each passing month.

"The cost is the bargain," she said. "The cost is always the bargain. The question is who pays it."

She picked up her pen. She wrote on the fresh sheet: *Priority: locate missing folios 1887-1892. Coordinate with Theo (county index), Dev (substation evidence), Yusuf (Warden records). Publication deadline: month's end. Complete record or no record.*

She signed it. She set the pen down.

"Let's go talk to Yusuf," she said. "He needs to know the gap is wider than we thought. The standing arrangement suspension changes the publication strategy."

Priya nodded. "I'll drive. You think."

They left the Archive together. The morning air was sharp, scented with pine and woodsmoke. The valley spread below them, ordinary and alive. Walt Pruitt raised a coffee mug from his porch. Odette flipped the sign in the general store window. *Open.*

Wren walked beside her friend, the missing folio's weight in her pocket, the gap in the record echoing behind them like a name she had once known but could no longer speak.


---

The drive to Millbrook took two hours if traffic held, and Theo drove like a man who had memorized every pothole and blind curve between the valley and the county seat. Wren sat in the passenger seat with her notebook open on her lap, pen uncapped but still. The scenery shifted from the valley's close green walls to wider fields, then the sprawl of strip malls and gas stations marking the edge of ordinary jurisdiction.

"You've been quiet," Theo said. His hands rested at ten and two on the steering wheel. Early morning light caught the edge of his jaw, the faint crease between his eyebrows.

"I'm thinking," Wren said. "About the folio. About what Adelaide wrote. About what Whitlock wrote. They're not the same story, but they're not different stories either. Two people looking at the same locked door from opposite sides."

Theo glanced over, brief. "And which side are you on?"

"Neither. I'm trying to find the hinges." She tapped the notebook. "Adelaide knew the bargain could be ended. She knew the cost. She chose not to pay it. Whitlock knew the same thing. He tried to pay it and failed. Then he hid what he knew instead of destroying it. Why?"

"Because destroying it wouldn't have worked." Theo's voice was flat, the tone he used when he was working. "Information doesn't die. It just waits for someone else to find it. He knew that. He was an archivist before he was anything else. He knew that hiding it in a locked box with a false bottom was safer than burning it, because fire leaves ash. Ash can be read."

"Is that what you think Drake found? Ash?"

"I think Drake found the box." Theo took the exit for Millbrook County Records, tires humming on the ramp. "And I think he's been reading the ash for nineteen years."

The county records building sat low and brutalist against the sky, concrete and glass that had looked modern in 1978 and looked tired now. Theo parked in the visitor lot and didn't reach for his badge. Didn't need it. The clerk at the front desk, a woman with gray hair pinned up and reading glasses on a chain, looked up from her crossword and smiled.

"Theo Marsh. Back again."

"Morning, Doris. This is Wren Castellano. She's with me."

Doris's eyes flicked to Wren, assessing. "Research?"

"Family history. On both sides."

"Well, you know where the reading room is. Box 47 is still in the restricted section. You'll need Yusuf's sign-off for that one."

Theo's jaw tightened. "Yusuf signed off last week. I have the email."

Doris didn't flinch. "Policy says I need to see it printed. Or you can wait while I call him."

"I have it on my phone."

"Policy says printed."

Theo exhaled through his nose. "I'll print it. Wren, the reading room is down the hall, second door on the left. I'll be five minutes."

Wren watched him walk toward the public terminals, his stride even, shoulders squared. She turned to Doris. "He comes here often?"

"Often enough. Since he was a boy, really. His grandmother used to bring him. Harriet Marsh. Sharp woman. Kept asking for records that didn't exist." Doris returned to her crossword. "Still does, far as I know. Comes in every few months. Asks for the same gap. Gets the same answer."

"The gap in the index."

"The standing arrangement notation. Restricted. Sealed. She can't let it go. He can't let it go." Doris didn't look up. "Stubborn runs in that family. So does grief."

Wren moved down the hall before the words could settle. The reading room smelled of paper dust and old leather, the dry scent of documents that had been waiting a long time to be read. Fluorescent lights hummed overhead. A single long table ran down the center, and at the far end, a gray archival box sat on a foam cradle. Box 47.

She pulled on nitrile gloves, the snap loud in the quiet. The lid lifted with a whisper of cardboard against cardboard. Inside, a folio - not a file, not a binder, a folio, hand-stitched leather cover cracked at the spine - rested on acid-free tissue. The leather was dark, nearly black, with no label on the cover. No accession number. No barcode.

Wren lifted it carefully. The stitching held. The pages inside were thick, handmade paper, the ink brown with age but legible. The first page bore a single line in a careful hand:

*Terms of the First Debt, as witnessed by Josiah Whitlock, Warden of the First Debt, Year 1847.*

Her breath caught. 1847. The valley's founding generation. The first renewal.

She turned the page. Dense script, margins filled with annotations in a different hand, lighter ink, finer pen. *Adelaide's hand,* she thought. *She read this. She annotated it.*

The text described the bargain not as a pact between the valley and some unnamed power, but as an administrative structure. A contract with terms, clauses, definitions. *Protection* defined as: mitigation of flood, blight, epidemic, fire. *Payment* defined as: memory, voluntarily given, proportionate to the gift exercised. *Debt* defined as: the cumulative weight of unpaid memory, transferable only by specific ritual witness.

*Transferable.* The word had been underlined twice. In the margin, Adelaide had written: *This is the crack. This is where it breaks.*

Wren turned pages. Accounts of renewals. Names. Dates. Amounts of memory taken. A ledger of loss. 1852: Margaret Kettering, three months of her daughter's first steps. 1868: Thomas Voss, the year he learned to read. 1891: Eleanor Whitlock - *Ambrose Whitlock's wife* - the memory of her wedding day.

*Ambrose Whitlock took his own wife's wedding day,* Wren thought. *To pay a debt he didn't owe.*

The annotations grew denser. Adelaide's handwriting cramped into the margins, arrows connecting entries, question marks, exclamation points. *He didn't have to. The debt wasn't his. He chose it. Why?*

Then a gap. Pages torn out. Clean edges, deliberate. The folio continued with a later hand - Whitlock's again, heavier, slower.

*I have hidden the complete terms. The ritual to end the bargain exists. I found it. I tried it. It requires a memory that no one person can carry. The cost is total. I will not ask anyone to pay it. I will not pay it myself. The bargain stands. The protection holds. But the truth must survive me.*

Below that, in Adelaide's hand: *You coward. You found the way and you buried it.*

And below that, in a third hand, block letters pressed hard enough to dent the paper: *DRAKE WAS HERE. 2005. HE READ THIS. HE TOOK THE MISSING PAGES.*

Wren's fingers went cold. 2005. Nineteen years ago. The Millbrook attempt.

She heard footsteps. Theo appeared in the doorway, a printed email in his hand. He looked at the folio in her lap, at her face, and came the rest of the way in.

"Doris was difficult," he said. "She wanted a wet signature. I told her the county chair's email authority supersedes desk policy. She told me to take it up with the state auditor. I printed the email anyway."

"She knows you."

"She knows my grandmother. She knows me by extension." He set the email on the table's edge, not touching the folio. "What did you find?"

"The original terms. Written by Josiah Whitlock. Annotated by Adelaide. And someone else." She pointed to the block letters. "Drake. He signed it. 2005."

Theo leaned over, reading. His face didn't change, but his hands, resting on the table, went still. "He was here. In this building. Reading this box. Nineteen years ago."

"The Millbrook attempt. He wasn't just trying to end the bargain there. He was researching it. He found Whitlock's hidden folio. He took the missing pages - the ritual, the complete terms - and left this note." Wren's voice was quiet. "He's had the core of it for nineteen years. The ritual to end the bargain. The true administrative terms. He just needed the rest."

"The rest being what?"

"The lineage records. The renewal ledgers. The names of every person who ever paid a debt, and what they lost." She closed the folio carefully. "He has the lock. He's been building the key."

Theo picked up the printed email, folded it slowly, put it in his pocket. "We need to photograph every page. High resolution. Both sides. The annotations especially. Then we need to get this back in the box and get out of here before someone notices we've opened Pandora's archive."

"Pandora didn't have a deadline."

"Drake gave us one." Theo pulled his phone from his pocket, unlocked it, and held it out to her. A text message, timestamped six minutes ago. No sender ID. Just a number.

*You have the Whitlock folio. You have thirty days from today to deliver the complete true record to me. After that, I publish my version. The valley's protection fails the moment the world knows the terms are a lie. Tick tock.*

Wren read it twice. Three times. The screen blurred.

"Thirty days," she said.

"Thirty days," Theo repeated. "From today. Which means he's watching. He knows we're here. He knows we have the folio."

"Or he's bluffing. Casting a wide net. Hoping someone bites."

"Drake doesn't bluff. He calculates. He's had nineteen years to calculate." Theo took the phone back, screen dark. "We publish first. Complete. True. Every name. Every debt. Every renewal. Before he can weaponize the partial version."

"Which means we have to finish the record. The parts Adelaide didn't have. The parts Whitlock hid. The parts Drake stole." Wren stood, the folio cradled against her chest. "We have to find the missing pages."

"Which are where?"

"With Drake. Or hidden somewhere he controlled in 2005. Millbrook. Or the valley. Or both." She moved toward the door, then stopped. "Theo. Your grandmother. Harriet. She lost ten years. The decade before 2007. That's 1997 to 2007. Drake's first attempt was nineteen years ago. 2005. Right inside her missing decade."

Theo didn't move. His face was careful, the expression he wore when he was filing something away for later. "Yes."

"She was in Millbrook when Drake made his attempt. She was there. She saw something. She knows something. And she can't remember it."

"She can't say where she was. She's told me that a hundred times. The memory isn't suppressed. It's gone. Taken." His voice was level, but his fingers tightened on the door frame. "That's why I came here. That's why I became an archivist. That's why I've spent fifteen years chasing gaps in county indexes. I'm not looking for the valley's secrets, Wren. I'm looking for my grandmother's life."

The words hung between them. The hum of the fluorescent lights. The weight of the folio in Wren's arms.

"I didn't know," she said. "I knew about the gap in the index. I knew you were investigating. I didn't know it was her. I didn't know it was personal."

"It's always personal. That's the thing about archives. People think they're about the past. They're not. They're about the people the past left behind." Theo pushed off the door frame. "Harriet Marsh. Seventy-five years old. Lives in an assisted living facility in Millbrook. Reads the same three mystery novels over and over because she forgets the endings. Remembers her wedding in 1971. Doesn't remember her granddaughter's birth in 1998. Doesn't remember her husband's death in 2003. Doesn't remember the decade between."

"Your sister?"

"Died in 2001. Car accident. Harriet doesn't remember the funeral. She asks about her sometimes. We tell her. She cries. Then she forgets again." He said it flat, like a case note. "Drake took that from her. Nineteen years ago. He took ten years of a woman's life and walked away. The Millbrook case went cold. No suspect. No charges. Just a gap in the record."

Wren set the folio on the reading room table, careful, precise. She reached for her notebook, flipped to a fresh page, and wrote: *Harriet Marsh. Memory loss 1997-2007. Drake's first attempt. Millbrook 2005. Gap in county index = standing arrangement notation.*

"Standing arrangement," she said. "That's what the restricted notation says. What does it mean?"

Theo came back to the table, picked up the folio, opened it to the first page. "It means someone made a deal with the county. A standing arrangement to restrict certain records. Permanent seal. No review date. No sunset clause. The only notation in the index is the phrase itself: *standing arrangement*. No name. No date. No authority cited."

"Who could authorize that?"

"County clerk. County judge. State archivist. Someone with the power to say *this never happened* and make it stick." Theo turned a page. "Nineteen years ago, someone with that power sealed the records of Drake's attempt. The investigation. The witness statements. The evidence. All of it. Standing arrangement. And my grandmother's missing decade sits right in the middle of it."

"Drake has friends in high places."

"Or he *is* the friend in high places. Information broker. He trades secrets. The standing arrangement might be his secret, traded for something else. Or it might be the price someone paid to keep his secret." Theo closed the folio. "Either way, the gap in the index is the thread. Pull it, and the whole thing unravels."

"Can we pull it? From here?"

"Not from here. The restricted section is physical. The original seal order is in a vault. I've requested it three times. Denied three times. *Standing arrangement*." The words tasted bitter. "But there's another way. The Millbrook attempt generated a police report. A case file. That's not county records. That's law enforcement. Different jurisdiction. Different retention schedule."

"Can we get it?"

"I've tried. Cold case. Sealed. Same wall." Theo set the folio back in its cradle. "But cold case files get reviewed. Every ten years. The next review is next month. If we can get a court order - "

"Thirty days, Theo. We have thirty days. A court order takes months."

"Then we don't use a court. We use a person." He looked at her, direct. "Priya Nair. Deputy. Caller lineage. She can compel truth within five feet. If we bring her to the Millbrook station, to the cold case reviewer - "

"She pays with her composure. Every use costs her. You know that."

"I know. She knows. But she also knows what Drake did. What he's doing. She'll do it." Theo's jaw set. "And Dev. Dev's evidence work collapsed Drake's leverage last time. He can do it again. He knows how to trace the paper trail from the missing pages to whatever Drake's built since."

"Dev served a sentence for redirecting tolls. He paid his debt. He's not - "

"He's a Finder. He's a Whitlock descendant. He's the reason the ring ritual worked. He's not sidelined, Wren. He's the sharpest edge we have." Theo's voice hardened. "And so are you. You're the one who can publish. You're the one the valley trusts. You're the one Drake is threatening."

Wren looked at the folio. At the leather cover cracked at the spine. At the ghost of Adelaide's handwriting in the margins. *This is the crack. This is where it breaks.*

"Thirty days," she said again. "To complete the record. To publish it. To beat Drake to the truth."

"To tell the truth before he sells the lie." Theo picked up the printed email from the table, tucked it into the folio's front cover. "We photograph every page. Now. Then we go back to the valley. We tell Yusuf. We tell Mara and Caleb. We tell Priya and Dev. We build the complete record from every source we have. And we publish."

"Thirty days," Wren repeated. She pulled her phone, opened the camera. "Let's start."

The work was slow. Deliberate. Each page photographed, checked, rephotographed if the glare caught wrong. Theo held the folio, turned pages with gloved fingers. Wren managed the lighting, the angles, the file naming convention. They didn't speak much. The silence was working silence, the kind that settles between people who know the same weight.

Around page forty, Wren paused. A page she'd missed in her first pass. Thin paper, different from the rest. Tipped in, not stitched. The handwriting was cramped, urgent, the ink faded to sepia.

*If you are reading this, you have found what I hid. Good. You deserve the truth. The bargain can be ended. The ritual requires ten witnesses, each carrying a portion of the final debt. The memory taken must be a memory of the bargain itself - the moment of renewal, the words spoken, the weight accepted. No single person holds this memory complete. It is distributed. The Warden of the First Debt knows the distribution. I was the Warden. I hid the distribution map in the valley's founding charter. It is written in the ink that fades. Look for the names that do not appear.*

Below that, in Adelaide's hand: *The ink that fades. The names that do not appear. He hid it in plain sight. In the charter we all signed and none of us read.*

And below that, in Drake's block letters: *FOUND THE CHARTER. 2005. TOOK THE MAP. THE DISTRIBUTION IS MINE.*

Wren lowered the camera. Her hands shook.

"The distribution map," she said. "The ring ritual. Dev's ring - ten volunteers. That was the distribution. Drake had the map. He knew how to break the bargain. He knew who to target."

"He didn't use it," Theo said quietly. "He had the map in 2005. He didn't use it then. He didn't use it last year. He's using it now. Thirty days. Publish or he publishes."

"Why wait nineteen years?"

"Because he didn't have the valley's trust. He didn't have access to the current renewals. He didn't have the names of the living debt-holders." Theo's voice was grim. "He has them now. Or he will. Thirty days is how long it takes him to verify the current distribution. To confirm the living Warden. To complete his version."

"Who's the current Warden?"

"The role is hereditary. Skips generations. Unpredictable. Dev is a Whitlock descendant. He might be the Warden. Or it might be someone else. Someone who doesn't know." Theo set the folio down. "We need to find out. Before Drake does."

Wren nodded, throat tight. She photographed the page. Then the next. And the next. The folio ended with a blank page, then a final notation in Adelaide's hand:

*I did not end the bargain. I chose the valley. I chose the people. I chose the protection. But I left the truth where it could be found. For the one who comes after. For the Finder who will not be protected from the truth.*

*For my granddaughter.*

The camera clicked. The flash washed out the words.

"Your grandmother," Theo said. Not a question.

"My grandmother. The Finder. She knew. She left this for me. She knew I would find it." Wren's voice cracked. "She protected me from the truth my whole life. And she left me the truth in a folio in a sealed box in a county records building two hours from home."

"She left you the choice," Theo said. "She left you the chance to decide what to do with it. That's not protection. That's trust."

"Is it? Or is it just another debt?"

Theo didn't answer. He packed the folio back into its box, replaced the lid, set it on the foam cradle exactly as they'd found it. Wren gathered her notebook, her pen, her phone. The reading room felt smaller now. The air thicker.

They walked out past Doris, who didn't look up from her crossword. The morning light hit the parking lot, harsh and bright. Theo's car sat where they'd left it. The drive back would be two hours. The valley waited.

"The standing arrangement," Wren said as they got in the car. "The restricted gap in the index. You've been chasing it for fifteen years. Since you were eleven."

"Since the summer Harriet stopped remembering. Since the Millbrook case went cold." Theo started the engine. "I thought if I found the gap, I'd find her. I'd find the decade. I'd give it back to her."

"Can you? Give it back?"

"No. Memory taken by the bargain doesn't return. Not whole. Not the same." He pulled out of the lot, merged onto the road. "But knowing *what* was taken. Knowing *why*. Knowing *who* took it. That's something. That's the only thing I can give her."

"Drake took it."

"Drake took it. Or someone working for him. Or someone he traded with. The standing arrangement seals the record of *who*." Theo's hands tightened on the wheel. "Thirty days, Wren. We find the record. We break the seal. We publish the truth. And maybe - maybe - Harriet Marsh gets to know what happened to her life."

The car picked up speed. The county buildings fell behind, then the strip malls, then the fields. The valley's green walls rose ahead, closer with every mile.

Wren watched the road unwind. Her notebook sat on her lap, closed. Her pen rested on the cover. Inside, the words waited: *Harriet Marsh. Memory loss 1997-2007. Drake's first attempt. Millbrook 2005. Gap in county index = standing arrangement notation. Thirty days.*

She thought of her own grandmother. The Finder who had protected her from truths. The Finder who had left her the folio, the map, the choice. *For the Finder who will not be protected from the truth.*

She thought of Theo's grandmother. Harriet Marsh, seventy-five, reading the same three mysteries, asking about a granddaughter she'd never forget if she could remember. A decade stolen. A case gone cold. A standing arrangement sealing the truth.

Two grandmothers. Two Finders. Two gaps.

Wren opened her notebook. Uncapped her pen. Wrote: *We are the ones who find what was lost. Even when the cost is everything.*

The car crested the rise. The valley spread below, green and quiet, the river catching the sun. Home.

Theo drove them down the winding road, toward the archive they would build, toward the truth they would tell, toward the thirty days that would decide everything.


---

The envelope arrived on a Tuesday, carried by a county courier who looked uncomfortable standing on the Reyes Orchard porch. Wren took it with her usual care, the paper thick and official, the seal unbroken. She had been expecting something like this since the first letter from Drake's attorney, but expectation did not make the weight of it lighter.

Theo stood beside her at the kitchen table, his coffee cooling in a ceramic mug. He had driven down from Millbrook that morning, the same rental car, the same careful parking at the end of the drive. The trip had become routine over the past six weeks, a rhythm of research and return that neither of them named.

"Court order," Wren said, turning the envelope over. "Millbrook County Superior Court. Judge Reyes. No relation to Caleb."

Theo set down his mug. "Reyes is a common name in the county. Three judges, two deputies, a coroner."

"Of course." She slid her finger under the flap. The paper inside was folded crisp, the ink sharp. She read it twice, the legal language dense but the directive clear.
