"And it says a ritual freed her. Split the weight. Ten people shared the cost." She tapped the page. "One of them was a Finder named Dev Nair. A descendant of Ambrose Whitlock. The practice runs in bloodlines, but it skips generations. Unpredictably."

"Yes."

Harriet's pale eyes held his. "The decade before 2007. That's when you were born. 1997. You were ten when I came back."

"I was."

"Your mother was pregnant with you in 1996. She was living in Millbrook. She worked at the library."

"She did."

"Did the valley take something from her too?"

Theo shook his head. "No. The valley's reach didn't extend to her. Drake's experiment was targeted. He needed a test subject with no connection to the valley, to prove the mechanism worked at a distance. He chose you because you were accessible, because you lived alone after Grandfather died, because your routines were predictable. It wasn't about your family. It was about geography."

"And the valley? The people who carry this bargain now? They didn't know?"

"They didn't know. Denise renewed in secret. The valley's protection operated on autopilot, drawing from the debt she paid. When the ring ritual happened, when the truth came out, they found you in the records. They found the extraction. They didn't know it had happened until they looked."

Harriet absorbed this. Her finger remained on the page. "So the valley didn't choose me. The valley didn't know me. A man named Drake chose me, and the valley's mechanism did what it was built to do, and nobody knew until a girl named Wren and a man named Theo dug it up."

"That's the shape of it."

"And now the world knows. The manuscript. The Archive. The county index. Everyone knows what the valley is, what it costs, who pays."

"Yes."

A long breath. Harriet closed the manuscript. She set it square on the table, aligned with the placemat, the napkin, the edge of the table itself. Order restored.

"Good," she said.

Theo blinked. "Good?"

"Good." She met his gaze, steady and clear. "Secrets rot. We both know that. Your grandfather knew it. Your mother knows it. You've built your life on digging them out. The valley kept its secret for generations, and look what happened. A man exploited it. A woman carried it alone for fifty years. A man lost two years of his life. Another man lost nineteen years of his grandmother." She paused. "Secrets rot. Truth preserves. Even when it hurts. Especially when it hurts."

The sentence hung between them, clean and sharp. One lucid, true sentence. The first she had spoken about the gap in nineteen years.

Theo felt something shift in his chest, a tectonic slide of understanding. All his life he had chased the shape of her absence, believing that if he could only document it, name it, prove it, the wound would close. He had thought transparency was the antidote to violation. He had thought the act of revelation was itself a kind of justice.

But he had been wrong. Transparency without consent was only exposure by another name. The valley had learned that the hard way. Wren had taught him. And now his grandmother, in a single sentence, had confirmed it.

Secrets rot. Truth preserves. But truth offered, not imposed. Truth consented to, not inflicted.

"You're right," Theo said. "I thought... I thought if I published it, if I made the record complete and public, it would fix something. For you. For the valley. For everyone who had pieces taken without asking. But publication isn't repair. It's accounting. The repair comes after. The repair is what people choose to do with the truth once they have it."

Harriet studied him. "And what will you choose?"

Theo considered the question. The manuscript on the table. The Archive waiting in the valley. Wren, somewhere in the records room, probably arguing with the binder about margin widths. The county index, finally complete. Drake in his cell. The valley's people living with their bargain, no longer in shadow, choosing each day whether the protection was worth the cost.

"I'll choose to listen," he said. "To the people the record names. To the people it doesn't. To the ones who want to read it and the ones who never will. I'll choose to let them decide what happens next. That's what transparency means. Not silence. Not exposure. Consent."

Harriet's smile this time was real, reaching her eyes, crinkling the corners. "You sound like your grandfather. He used to say the only thing harder than keeping a secret was knowing when to tell it."

"Did he tell you when?"

"He told me when I asked. And sometimes when I didn't, but he could see I needed to hear it." She pushed the manuscript toward him, across the laminated placemat. "Take it with you. I don't need to read the rest. I know the shape of it. I know the man's name. I know the valley's name. I know my grandson did the work."

"You don't want to keep it?"

"I want you to keep it. You're the archivist. You're the one who files things where they belong." She patted the cover once, a benediction. "Put it on your shelf. Next to the sourdough recipe. Next to the funeral program. Next to the takeout menus. Someday, when I'm gone, you'll know where to find it. And if I ask you to read me a passage before then, you will. But only if I ask."

Theo gathered the pages, jogging them straight, securing the clip. The paper was warm now, holding the heat of her hands. "I will."

"Good." Harriet folded her hands on the table, the picture of composure. "Now. Tell me about this girl. The Finder. Does she make you happy?"

Theo felt his face warm. "She does. Very."

"Does she have spirit?"

"More than anyone I've ever met."

"Good. That's what matters. Spirit and honesty. The rest is paperwork." She glanced at the window, where the light had turned gold and long. "Your mother is coming for dinner on Sunday. She said to tell you. She's making the roast. The one with the onions you like."

"I'll be there."

"See that you are. And bring the girl. I want to meet the woman who taught my grandson the difference between finding and taking."

Theo stood, the chair legs scraping on the linoleum. He slung his bag over his shoulder, the manuscript secure inside. "I'll tell her. She'll want to meet you too."

"Of course she will. She's a Finder. Finding people is what she does." Harriet rose as well, slower, one hand on the table for balance. She was smaller than he remembered, or perhaps he had simply grown taller in the years since he last stood over her. "Walk me to my room. The nurse with the loud shoes will be back soon, and I'd rather not have the spirit conversation twice."

They walked down the corridor together, past doors marked with names and small decorations, a wreath here, a photograph there. The air smelled of antiseptic and lemon polish. Harriet moved at her own pace, unhurried, and Theo matched her stride without thinking.

At her door, she turned. The hallway light caught the silver of her hair, the blue of her eyes.

"Theo."

"Yes, Grandmother."

"I'm proud of you. Not for the manuscript. Not for the Archive. Not for catching the man." She touched his cheek, her fingers cool and dry. "I'm proud of you because you learned to listen. That's the thing that changes everything. The rest is just paper."

She went inside, the door clicking shut behind her. Theo stood in the hallway for a long moment, the manuscript weight against his hip, the echo of her words settling into the space where the knot used to be.

Secrets rot. Truth preserves. Consent decides.

He turned toward the exit, the gravel drive, the highway that would take him back to the valley, to Wren, to the Archive where the complete record would sit on shelves built by hands that knew the cost of every page. The sun was low now, painting the hills in amber and rose. The oak tree at the facility's entrance stood bare against the sky, its branches reaching, patient, rooted in ground that remembered every season.

Theo got in his car. He started the engine. He drove.


---

The county courthouse in Millbrook smelled of floor wax and old paper, the same scent that had greeted Theo every Tuesday for the past year when he drove up from the valley to sit in the records room. Today the room held more people than the fire code probably allowed. Yusuf sat in the front row beside a woman Theo had never met but recognized from photographs - Adelaide's daughter, the one who had inherited the letters. Priya stood near the door with her arms crossed, her uniform crisp, her face unreadable. Dev sat two rows back, a stack of folders on his lap. Wren sat beside Theo, her notebook closed, her pen resting on top of it. She had not opened it once since they walked in.

Corwin Drake stood at the podium where the county clerk usually presided. He wore a charcoal suit that cost more than Theo's car, and his hair was trimmed to a neatness that suggested a barber visited his home. He did not look like a man who had spent nineteen years chasing a ghost through county archives and forgotten ledgers. He looked like a man who had found what he was looking for and was now negotiating the price.

"Mr. Marsh," Drake said. His voice carried without amplification. "You've had the complete file for three weeks. Your investigator's credentials gave you access to the restricted index. The standing arrangement notation. The gap in the 1984 renewal. You know what the valley is. You know what it costs. And you know what happens if I release my version first."

Theo did not stand. He did not look at Wren. He kept his eyes on Drake.

"I know what you have," Theo said. "A partial record. Seventeen renewals documented from the outside. Names, dates, the memories taken. You have the ledger pages Ambrose Whitlock hid in his family's possession. You have the correspondence between Whitlock and Adelaide Reyes. You have the county index entries I helped you cross-reference before I understood what they meant. What you don't have is the context. The reasons. The choice."

"Context is what people use to excuse the inexcusable," Drake said. "The valley trades memory for protection. That's the headline. That's what the county board will see. That's what the state investigators will see when I hand them the file next Monday. Unless - "

"Unless what?" Theo asked.

Drake smiled. It was a thin expression, the kind that belonged in a boardroom. "Unless you deliver the complete original terms. The bargain's administrative structure. The mechanism. The names of every current Warden and Finder and Caller. The operational details that would let me - let us - assume the agreement ourselves. A clean transfer. No one needs to know the valley exists. The protection continues. The debt continues. Only the management changes."

The room was very quiet. Theo heard the hum of the building's ancient radiator. He heard Wren's breath, steady and slow.

"And if I refuse," Theo said.

"Then I publish on Monday," Drake said. "My version. The damning version. The one that says Amity Falls is a cult that steals memories from its own children. The one that triggers a state investigation, a federal inquiry, a media circus that will destroy every life in that valley before the week is out. Your grandmother's name will be in the first paragraph. Harriet Marsh. Memory loss, 1997 to 2007. Cause unknown. The county's own case file references an 'unexplained neurological event.' I have the medical records. I have the police report from the night she was found wandering Route 9 with no memory of the previous decade. I have the doctor's note suggesting environmental exposure. I have the sealed testimony from the investigator who quit two weeks later and moved to Arizona. All of it fits a pattern. My pattern."

Theo's hands rested on his knees. He had read those documents. He had held his grandmother's medical file in his hands three weeks ago, the paper thin as tissue, the ink faded to sepia. He had sat in the Millbrook records room for six hours straight, cross-referencing Drake's timeline with the valley's renewal dates. The match was exact. Every gap in Harriet's memory aligned with a renewal Drake had documented. The valley had taken ten years from her, piece by piece, and no one had told her why. No one had told Theo why. He had built his career on the absence of that answer.

"She doesn't know," Theo said. "She can't tell you where she was. She doesn't remember the valley. She doesn't remember the bargain. She only knows she lost time."

"Which makes her the perfect witness," Drake said. "A victim who cannot defend the people who hurt her. The press loves a sympathetic victim. So do prosecutors."

Theo stood up. The chair scraped the floor, loud in the silence.

"No," Theo said.

Drake's smile faltered. "Excuse me?"

"I said no," Theo said. "I'm not giving you the terms. I'm not giving you the mechanism. I'm not giving you the names. You want control of the bargain? You don't get it. Not through me. Not through leverage. Not through threats."

"You'll let the valley burn?" Drake asked. "You'll let your grandmother's story become a headline? You spent nineteen years looking for the truth. You finally found it. And now you're going to walk away because you don't like the shape of it?"

"I spent nineteen years looking for what happened to her," Theo said. "I found it. The answer is ugly. The answer is that the valley took her memories to pay a debt she didn't agree to owe. The answer is that the people who did it believed they had no choice. The answer is that they were wrong. But the answer is also that they built something that has protected hundreds of people for generations. Children who didn't drown in floods. Families who didn't starve when crops failed. Elderly who didn't die of pneumonia in winters that should have killed them. That protection is real. The cost is real. And the choice - the choice to keep going, or to end it, or to change it - belongs to the people who live there. Not to you. Not to me. Not to any outsider with a file and a deadline."

Drake's face hardened. "You're naive. You think the world works on consent. You think a community built on a hidden bargain can survive exposure. You think the county board will care about nuances when they see 'memory theft' in a police report. By Monday morning, this room will be full of reporters. By Wednesday, the state police will be at the valley's edge. By Friday, every lineage member will be in custody. Is that what you want? Is that what you've been working for?"

"I've been working for the truth," Theo said. "The whole truth. Not your version. Not the valley's version. The truth that includes what the bargain costs and what it buys and who decided it was worth it. The truth that includes Ambrose Whitlock's letter admitting he tried to end it and failed. The truth that includes Adelaide's letter naming him as her teacher. The truth that includes the ring ritual, and Denise's fifty years, and Caleb's restored memory, and the fact that the valley found a way to distribute the weight instead of letting one person carry it all. That truth exists. I have it. Wren has it. We've been assembling it for a year."

"You have a manuscript," Drake said. "A draft. Unpublished. Unverified. My file is documented. Sourced. Dated. Cross-referenced with county records. It goes to the press Monday. Your draft goes nowhere unless I allow it."

"Then you don't understand what we've built," Theo said.

He reached into his bag and pulled out a flash drive. Small, black, unmarked. He held it up so the room could see.

"This contains the complete record," Theo said. "Every renewal. Every memory taken. Every name. Every date. Every letter. Every ledger page. The original terms as Whitlock wrote them. The administrative structure as Adelaide documented it. The lineage lists. The debt ledger. The ring ritual transcript. The medical records of every person who paid a debt, including Harriet Marsh. All of it. Sourced. Verified. Cross-referenced. Three copies exist. One with me. One with Wren. One with the county clerk's office, filed this morning under a restricted access order that expires in forty-eight hours unless a judge extends it. Which means by Monday morning, the complete true history of Amity Falls will be a matter of public record. Filed by me. Authored by the valley. Not by you."

The silence stretched. Drake stared at the flash drive. His fingers curled around the podium.

"You filed it," Drake said. "This morning."

"Eight thirty," Theo said. "Before this meeting. The clerk's office has a timestamp. The file is encrypted. The key is held by three people. Me. Wren. The county clerk. If anything happens to any of us, the key releases automatically. If you publish your version, the complete record publishes simultaneously. Side by side. The world gets both. They can compare. They can decide."

"That's blackmail," Drake said.

"That's insurance," Theo said. "You wanted leverage. You thought you had the only copy of the truth. You don't. You never did. The valley kept its own records. We just spent the last year organizing them."

Drake was quiet for a long moment. Then he laughed, short and sharp. "You think this changes anything. You think the truth protects anyone. The truth is what destroys them. You've just guaranteed the valley's exposure. You've done my work for me."

"No," Theo said. "I've done the work the valley asked me to do. A year ago, Wren came to me and asked if I would help them tell their own story. Not hide it. Not spin it. Tell it. Honestly. Completely. On their terms. I said yes. Not because I agreed with everything they've done. I don't. I said yes because the alternative - the alternative is what you're doing. Someone else telling the story. Someone with an agenda. Someone who doesn't care who gets hurt. I've seen what that looks like. I've lived with it for nineteen years. My grandmother lost ten years of her life because someone with an agenda decided the truth was a weapon. I will not be that person. And I will not help you be that person."

Drake's jaw tightened. "And the bargain? The terms? The mechanism? You're just going to put it all in the public record? The administrative structure that lets the valley function? The names of every current lineage member? You'll destroy the protection to save the truth?"

"The protection survives exposure," Theo said. "Or it doesn't. That's not my decision. That's the valley's decision. They get to make it now. With all the facts. For the first time in two hundred years, they get to choose whether the bargain continues, or ends, or changes. They get to choose together. That's what the ring ritual was about. That's what Denise's freedom was about. That's what every conversation in that valley for the past year has been about. Choice. Real choice. Informed choice. You want to take that away from them. I won't let you."

"And if they choose to end it?" Drake asked. "If the truth breaks the bargain? If the protection fails? The floods come back. The blight returns. The sickness spreads. Children die. Is that on you?"

Theo looked at Wren then. She was watching him, her face calm, her hands folded in her lap. She gave him a small nod.

"If they choose to end it," Theo said, "they choose to end it. That's their right. It's always been their right. The bargain was never a suicide pact. It was an agreement. Agreements can be renegotiated. They can be dissolved. But only by the people who made them. Not by an information broker with a deadline."

Drake studied Theo's face. Then he looked at Wren. Then at Yusuf. Then at Priya. Then at Dev. His gaze moved across the room, taking in the valley's representatives - the council chair, the deputy, the Finder who had broken the oldest taboo and lived, the woman who had carried the bargain alone for fifty years and walked out of the ring ritual alive.

"You've all decided," Drake said. "You're going to publish. All of it. The names. The costs. The mechanism. Everything."

"We are," Yusuf said. His voice was quiet but it carried. "The valley voted. Three weeks ago. Unanimous. Every adult resident. They chose transparency. They chose to trust their neighbors with the truth. They chose to stop hiding."

"Even knowing what it costs?" Drake asked. "Even knowing the world will come for you?"

"Especially knowing that," Wren said. She had stood up. Theo hadn't heard her move. She stood beside him now, close enough that he felt the warmth of her shoulder against his arm. "Hiding didn't protect us. It just meant we couldn't defend ourselves. You proved that. Your file - your partial file - would have destroyed us because we had no answer. Now we do. The answer is the truth. The whole truth. Not your selection. Not our secrets. All of it."

Drake looked between them. Something shifted in his expression - calculation, reassessment, the cold recalibration of a man watching his leverage evaporate.

"Monday," Drake said. "I still publish Monday. My file goes to the press. Your file sits in the clerk's office. The press gets my version first. By the time yours is decrypted and verified, the narrative is set. The valley is a cult. The lineages are criminals. The bargain is a crime. You understand how media works. First story wins."

"Your file is incomplete," Dev said. He had stood too, moving to stand beside Priya. "It references the 1984 renewal as a gap. It doesn't name the Finder who paid that debt. It doesn't name the Warden who authorized it. It doesn't include the ledger page showing the debt was redirected. I have that page. I found it in the substation evidence locker three months ago. It names Ambrose Kell. It names the Finder he coerced. It names the memory taken - Caleb Reyes's two years. My file includes the signed confession Kell wrote before he fled. It includes the forensic analysis proving the redirect. It includes the medical records showing Caleb's memory restoration after the ring ritual. Your file has a gap. Mine fills it. When both files publish, the gap becomes the story. Not the valley. You."

Drake's eyes narrowed. "You have the Kell confession."

"I have the original," Dev said. "Handwritten. Witnessed by Priya. Filed with the county prosecutor's office last week. Kell is a fugitive. His confession reopens the Millbrook case from nineteen years ago. The one you were tied to. The one that went cold. The prosecutor's office is very interested in new evidence."

The color drained from Drake's face. He masked it quickly, but Theo saw it. The tightening at the jaw. The flicker of something behind the eyes.

"That case was closed," Drake said. "No charges filed. Insufficient evidence."

"Insufficient evidence then," Dev said. "Sufficient evidence now. The confession names you. Not by name - Kell didn't know your name. But by description. Information broker. Partial records. Nineteen years assembling a file. The prosecutor's office has a description. They have a timeline. They have the Millbrook police report from the night Harriet Marsh was found. The officer who took the report noted a man watching from a dark sedan. License plate partial. Make and model. The DMV cross-reference yields one registered owner in the tri-state area matching that description. Corwin Drake. Age forty-seven. Address in Hartford. Business license for 'Drake Archival Services.' The prosecutor's office made the connection two days ago. They're waiting for the file publication to move forward."

Drake was very still. "You've been busy."

"We've all been busy," Priya said. "Dev's evidence work. Wren's documentation. Theo's research. My testimony. Yusuf's council records. Odette's store ledgers. Denise's journals. Mara's engineering reports. Caleb's orchard records. The valley documented itself. Thoroughly. Methodically. Legally. You came here with a threat. We answered with a record."

Drake looked at the flash drive in Theo's hand. Then at the faces around the room. The calculation returned, but it was different now - defensive, not offensive.

"If I walk away," Drake said, "if I don't publish - what happens to me?"

"Nothing," Theo said. "You walk away. Your file stays unpublished. The valley's file publishes on schedule. The prosecutor's office gets Dev's evidence. The Millbrook case reopens. You get a lawyer. You face the consequences of whatever you did nineteen years ago. Or you don't. That's between you and the law. But the valley - the valley gets to tell its own story. On its own terms. Without you."

"And if I publish anyway?"

"Then both files publish," Theo said. "Side by side. The world sees your partial record and our complete one. They see the gaps you left. They see the evidence you ignored. They see the confession you didn't know existed. They see the valley's choice to be transparent. They decide. But either way, you lose control. You lose the leverage. You lose the narrative. The only question is whether you go down fighting or you walk away now."

Drake was quiet for a long time. The radiator hummed. Someone coughed in the back row. Wren's fingers brushed Theo's wrist - light, brief, a contact that lasted less than a second but anchored him more than anything else in the room.

"You're offering me nothing," Drake said finally. "No deal. No concession. No guarantee."

"I'm offering you the truth," Theo said. "That's all I have to offer. That's all any of us have. You wanted the bargain's terms? They're in the file. You wanted the mechanism? It's in the file. You wanted the names? They're in the file. You wanted control? You don't get that. No one does. Not anymore."

Drake exhaled slowly. His shoulders dropped a fraction. When he spoke again, his voice was flatter, stripped of the performative charm.

"Monday," he said. "I'll decide by Monday."

"There is no Monday," Theo said. "The file publishes Friday. The clerk's order expires Friday at five p.m. The encryption key releases automatically. The complete record goes live on the county server, mirrored to three news outlets, two university archives, and the Library of Congress. That's the deadline. Not yours. Ours."

Drake nodded once. A short, sharp movement. "Friday. Five p.m. I'll be gone before then."

He gathered his briefcase. He did not look at any of them as he walked out. The door closed behind him with a solid thunk.

The room exhaled.

Yusuf let out a breath that sounded like a groan. He rubbed his face with both hands. "God. I thought he was going to - I didn't think - "

"He folded," Priya said. She sounded almost disappointed. "I wanted him to push. I had a whole speech prepared."

"He didn't fold," Dev said. "He recalculated. He'll try something else. Before Friday. He has to."

"Let him try," Wren said. "We've got the record. We've got the evidence. We've got the prosecutor. We've got each other. What else does he have?"

Theo looked at her. The fluorescent lights caught the bridge of her nose, the curve of her jaw, the dark fall of her hair. She looked tired. They all looked tired. But she also looked steady in a way she hadn't a year ago, or six months ago, or even three weeks ago when they had started the final assembly.

"What else does he have?" Theo repeated softly.

"Nothing that matters," Wren said. She met his eyes. "Nothing we can't answer."

The tension in Theo's chest, knotted since the day he opened the restricted index and saw the standing arrangement notation, began to loosen. Not gone. Not resolved. But loosened. The leverage had shifted. The threat had been named and met. The valley's story was no longer a weapon in someone else's hand.

"Friday at five," Theo said. "The file goes live. The Archive opens. The valley tells its truth."

"Friday at five," Wren agreed.

She reached for his hand. Her fingers curled around his, warm and dry and certain. Theo closed his hand over hers and held on.

Outside the courthouse windows, the Millbrook afternoon was gray and bright, the sky a flat silver that promised snow. The town moved on the streets below - ordinary people, ordinary lives, no memory of bargains or debts or the weight of hidden things. Theo watched them for a moment, then turned back to the room, to the people who had become his people, to the work that waited.

"Let's go home," he said.


---

The kitchen table held three coffee mugs and a stack of printed pages, corners softened from shuffling and re-shuffling. Morning light cut across the scratched surface at a slant that made dust motes visible, each one catching and releasing as Wren turned a page.

Theo sat across from her, his copy open to the same section. He hadn't touched his coffee in twenty minutes. The steam had stopped rising.

"You want me to answer it now," he said.

"I want you to answer it honestly." Wren kept her voice level. She had practiced the question in the shower, on the walk from the orchard, standing at the sink washing a single spoon. "If the county clerk's office asked for the full record tomorrow - not the redacted version, not the summary Yusuf approved for the public meeting - the whole thing, every name, every debt paid, every memory taken - would you hand it over?"

Theo didn't look up. His fingers rested on the table beside the manuscript, nails trimmed short, knuckles pale.

"Would you?" she asked.

He exhaled. The sound was quiet in the kitchen, swallowed by the refrigerator's hum. "You're asking if I'd violate the agreement we made with Yusuf. With the council. With every person in this valley who trusted us with their stories."

"I'm asking if you'd violate it," she said. "Not if you should. Not if it's legal. If the request came through proper channels, with a subpoena or a court order or whatever piece of paper makes it official - would you give them what they asked for?"

Theo looked up then. His eyes were the color of river water in late autumn, gray-green and depthless. Circles beneath them hadn't been there three weeks ago, before the first draft of Drake's manuscript arrived in a plain envelope with no return address.

"That's not a fair question," he said.

"Then don't answer it like a fair question. Answer it like the one I actually asked."

He was silent long enough that Wren heard the orchard sprinklers kick on outside, the hiss and thunk of water hitting aluminum pipe. Caleb's system, upgraded last spring. She had helped him test the pressure valves.

"My grandmother," Theo said finally. "Harriet. She lost ten years. The decade before 2007. She was forty-eight when it happened. Fifty-eight when she came back to herself. She doesn't know where she was. She doesn't know who she talked to. She doesn't know if she signed something, if she said something, if she *did* something. Nineteen years I've been looking. Nineteen years of FOIA requests and court petitions and quiet conversations with clerks who remembered her name. And every time - every single time - someone decided what she was allowed to know. Someone decided the truth was too dangerous. Too disruptive. Too *inconvenient*."

His voice dropped, roughened at the edges.

"So when you ask me if I'd hand over the full record to a government office - you're asking me if I'd do to this valley what was done to my family. You're asking me if I'd be the person who decides someone else doesn't get to know their own life."

Wren's throat tightened. She had known the shape of his wound. She hadn't known the weight of it pressing against this moment.

"I'm not asking you to be that person," she said. "I'm asking if you *are* that person. When the pressure comes. When the law says you have to choose."

Theo's jaw worked. "The law doesn't say I have to choose. The law says I have to comply. There's a difference."

"Is there?"

"Yes. There is." He stood abruptly, the chair legs scraping the floor. He moved to the window, shoulders rigid beneath his flannel shirt. "I've spent my adult life learning how systems protect themselves. How they bury things. How they rename suppression 'procedure' and call it justice. I know exactly what happens when a county clerk's office gets a complete record of something they don't understand. They don't study it. They don't preserve it. They classify it. They restrict it. They make it disappear into a drawer that nobody's allowed to open - not the people it belongs to, not the researchers, not even the judges half the time. So no. I would not hand it over. Not voluntarily. Not if I had any choice at all."

Wren nodded once. The motion felt small, insufficient. "And if you didn't have a choice?"

Theo turned. His face was drawn, the lines around his mouth deeper than she remembered from the cataloguing sessions in August, from the evenings on the porch with Caleb's cider and Mara's terrible cookies. "Then I'd make sure the version they got was the one *we* wrote. The one with context. The one that explains what a debt *is* before it lists who paid it. The one that says this valley isn't a case study or a cautionary tale or a supernatural anomaly - it's a community. People who love each other. People who make sacrifices for each other. People who *chose* this, even the ones who didn't know they were choosing."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only answer I have." He came back to the table but didn't sit. He braced his hands on the back of his chair. "Wren, what do you want me to say? That I'll burn the manuscript before I let anyone see it? That I'll lie under oath? That I'll - what, flee to another state with the only copy in my trunk? I'm an archivist. My whole profession is built on the premise that records survive. That truth outlasts the people who try to bury it. But I also know that *how* truth survives matters. *Who* tells it matters. *When* it's told matters."

"Drake is telling it," she said. "Right now. His version. Partial. Damning. Missing the parts that make it human."

"I know."

"He's going to publish in three weeks. His publisher has the galley proofs. The marketing team has the advance copy blurbs. 'The Secret History of Amity Falls: Memory, Power, and the Town That Forgot Itself.' That's the working title. His agent told a reporter at the *County Gazette* it's the most important regional exposé in twenty years."

Theo's expression didn't change. "I know."

"So we publish first. Our version. Complete. Honest. With every name and every debt and every memory - *except* the ones Yusuf and the council said stay redacted. The ones that belong to people who are still alive and didn't consent to public exposure. We give the world the truth *with* the context. We give the valley the protection *of* the context."

"That's the plan. That's been the plan since September."

"Then why does it feel like we're arguing about something else?"

Because they were. She knew it. He knew it. The question about consent had never been about a hypothetical subpoena. It had been about the night two weeks ago when she found him in the archive room at 2 a.m., the restricted files spread across the reading table, his phone recording page after page of the unredacted manuscript - the version only she and Yusuf and Priya were supposed to access. He had said he was checking cross-references. He had said he needed to verify the timeline on the 1987 renewal. He had said a lot of things.

She hadn't said anything. She had walked out. She hadn't come back to the archive for three days.

"You took pictures," she said now. "Of the unredacted pages. The ones with Odette's proposal memory. The ones with Denise's renewal records. The ones with my grandmother's name in the Finder ledger from 1972. You took pictures of things you promised you wouldn't photograph."

Theo went very still. "I took notes. For the footnotes. For the appendix on methodology. So the record shows *how* we reached our conclusions."

"Notes don't have metadata. Notes don't have timestamps and geotags. Notes don't get uploaded to a cloud backup you forgot to disable." Her voice cracked on the last word. She steadied it. "I checked your phone, Theo. When you left it on the charger in the kitchen. I didn't mean to. The screen lit up with a sync notification. Google Photos. Two hundred forty-seven images. Dated October 14th. 2:13 a.m. to 3:47 a.m."

The silence stretched. The sprinklers cycled off outside. Somewhere in the house, a floorboard settled.

"I didn't upload them," he said quietly. "The backup is automatic. I turned it off the next morning. I deleted the cloud copies. I deleted the local copies. The only version that exists now is the one on the secure drive in my bag - the one encrypted to the same standard the county uses for witness protection files. I *told* you I was verifying the timeline. I *did* verify the timeline. The 1987 renewal has a discrepancy in the witness signatures that changes the chain of custody for the memory ledger. It matters for the appendix. It matters for the historical record."

"It matters for *your* record," she said. "The one you're building in your head. The one where you're the person who finally gets it right. The one where you make sure no grandmother ever loses ten years without someone being held accountable."

"That's not fair."

"Neither was taking pictures of Odette's proposal memory. She lost the *words*, Theo. Forty-three years ago. She kept the joy. She told us that. She *trusted* us with that. And you photographed the page that says exactly which memory was taken and exactly when and exactly who authorized it - *me*. My signature. My handwriting. Because I was the Finder on duty that week. Because I was the one who located the memory in the archive. Because I was the one who handed it to the Warden."

Theo's face went pale. "Wren - "

"I was nineteen. I didn't know what I was signing. I thought it was a routine retrieval. I thought the memory was *lost* - a child's toy, a wedding ring, a set of keys. That's what the request form said. 'Locate missing item: proposal memory, Odette Reyes, 1979.' I found it. I brought it. I signed the release. I didn't ask why a proposal memory needed to be located. I didn't ask who was asking. I didn't ask *why*." She stopped, breathing hard. "That page - the one you photographed - it has my name on it. My signature. The date. The notation: *debt satisfied per standing arrangement.* That's *my* debt. A small one. A note in a drawer. A thank-you that puzzled me. I didn't even remember paying it until I saw it in the ledger last year. And you - you took a picture of it. For your appendix. For your methodology. For *your* truth."

"I didn't know," he said. "I didn't connect - I saw the discrepancy in the 1987 witness signatures. I was following the thread. I wasn't looking for your name. I wasn't looking for *anyone's* name. I was looking for the gap in Drake's account. The place where his partial record falls apart. Dev found it in the substation evidence. The exact gap. The missing renewal. The one that proves Drake doesn't have the complete terms. I needed to document *why* that gap exists. How the record-keeping failed. How the standing arrangement notation was used to hide - "

"To hide *me*," she said. "To hide what I did. What I didn't know I did. You photographed my ignorance, Theo. You photographed my complicity. And you didn't tell me. You didn't ask. You just - took it. Because the record matters more than the person."

"That's not true."

"Then why didn't you tell me? Why didn't you come to me *before* you opened the restricted files? Why didn't you say, 'Wren, I found something with your name on it, I need to understand it, can we look together?' Why did you wait until 2 a.m. when nobody was watching?"

His hands gripped the chair back until the knuckles whitened. "Because I was afraid. Because every time I get close to something that matters - something that explains what happened to my grandmother, something that might *help* - someone decides I don't need to know. Someone decides the truth is too dangerous. Someone decides *for* me. And I swore - I *swore* - that I would never be that person. That I would never withhold. That I would never protect someone by lying to them. So when I found the discrepancy, when I saw the thread pulling toward something real, I followed it. I followed it the way I've followed every thread for nineteen years. Alone. In the dark. Because that's the only way I know how to be sure nobody's hiding anything from me."

Wren stood. Her chair scraped back. She moved to the counter, poured the cold coffee down the sink, ran the water until it ran clear. Her hands shook. She gripped the counter edge.

"You hid it from me," she said to the running water. "You hid what you were doing. You hid what you found. You hid the pictures. That's not following a thread. That's *burying* it. That's exactly what you swore you wouldn't do."

The water shut off. Theo didn't answer.

She turned. He was still by the chair, shoulders hunched, the morning light catching the silver at his temples. He looked tired. He looked like he had looked the first time she met him, standing at the county index terminal with his careful notes and his careful questions, asking for a record that didn't exist.

"I'm not my grandmother," she said. "And you're not the clerk who denied her. And this valley isn't Millbrook. But you acted like it was. You acted like the only way to get the truth was to take it - to take *from* the people who trusted you with it. And that - that's the thing I can't get past. Not the pictures. Not the restricted files. The *taking*. The assumption that your need for the complete record outweighs my right to know you're looking at my name in a ledger I didn't know existed."

"I didn't - " He stopped. Swallowed. "I didn't assume that. I was *terrified* of that. I was terrified that if I asked, you'd say no. That Yusuf would say no. That the council would say no. That someone would decide the discrepancy wasn't relevant, or wasn't safe, or wasn't *necessary* - and I would lose the only chance to prove Drake wrong before he publishes. I was terrified of losing the truth *again*."

"And I was terrified of losing you," she said. "To the truth. To the version of you that decides the record matters more than the people in it. I've been terrified of that since the first week. Since you asked for the standing arrangement notation and I didn't know if I was allowed to give it to you. Since I realized you'd keep asking until someone stopped you - or until you stopped yourself. And you didn't stop. You *took*."

The word hung between them. Heavy. Final.

Theo's breath came shallow. "What do you want from me, Wren?"

The question landed in the space where her anger had been, and something shifted. Cracked open. The rage she had been carrying for two weeks - the betrayal, the violation, the cold knot in her chest every time she saw his phone on the charger - it didn't vanish. But it made room for something else.

She knew this feeling. She had felt it when her father left. When her grandmother died. When Caleb's memory returned and she realized how much she had been protected from. The feeling of naming a loss while it was still happening.

"I want you to ask," she said. "Next time. The time after that. Every time. I want you to come to me and say, 'Wren, I found something. It involves you. Can we look at it together?' I want you to trust that I can handle the truth about myself - even the ugly parts, even the parts I didn't choose. I want you to trust that *I get to decide* what happens to my name in your record. Not you. Not the county. Not Drake. *Me*."

Theo was silent. His throat moved.

"And I want you to tell me," she continued, her voice gaining strength, "that you understand why the redactions exist. Not why they're convenient. Not why they're strategic. Why they *exist*. Because every redacted name in that manuscript is a person who didn't consent to be a case study. Odette. Denise. My grandmother. Dev. Priya. My *mother*. They trusted us with their memories. They trusted *me* with their memories. And if you hand the unredacted version to a county clerk - or a publisher, or a journalist, or anyone with a subpoena and a budget - you're not just releasing information. You're releasing *them*. You're saying their consent doesn't matter because the record matters more."

"I know that," he said. "I *know* that. Wren - I didn't take the pictures to publish them. I didn't take them to give to Drake. I didn't take them to give to *anyone*. I took them because I needed to *see* the complete picture. To understand how the pieces fit. To make sure our version - the one we publish - is *stronger* than his. More complete. More honest. More *defensible*. I thought - I thought if I had the full context, I could build the argument that protects everyone. That the redactions aren't gaps in the truth, they're *part* of the truth. The part that says: this belongs to the people who lived it. Not to the archive. Not to the public. Not to history."

"Then say it," she said. "Say it out loud. Say: 'The redactions are part of the truth. The consent is part of the record. I will not violate them.' Say it like you mean it. Like it's not a negotiation."

Theo was quiet for a long moment. His eyes held hers, gray-green and raw.

"The redactions are part of the truth," he said. "The consent is part of the record. I will not violate them."

"Again."

"The redactions are part of the truth. The consent is part of the record. I will not violate them."

"One more time. Looking at me."

He didn't look away. "The redactions are part of the truth. The consent is part of the record. I will not violate them. I'm sorry, Wren. I'm sorry I took the pictures. I'm sorry I didn't tell you. I'm sorry I made you find out this way. I'm sorry I acted like my fear justified betraying your trust. It didn't. It doesn't. I was wrong."

The words landed in the kitchen, one after another, each one heavier than the last. Wren felt something in her chest loosen - not heal, not fix, but *loosen*, like a knot worked free by patient fingers.

"I believe you," she said. "I believe you're sorry. I believe you understand. But - " She stopped. Swallowed. "But I also need you to understand that sorry doesn't undo it. The pictures existed. The cloud backup happened. The metadata exists somewhere on Google's servers, probably forever. You can't un-take them. You can't un-know what you saw. And I can't un-know that you chose to take them instead of asking me."

"I know."

"And I need you to know - " Her voice cracked. She pressed on. "I need you to know that I *want* this. Us. The work. The publication. The life where you're in my kitchen arguing about archival methodology at 7 a.m. and I'm making terrible coffee and you're drinking it anyway. I want that. I've wanted it since September. Since the first cataloguing session when you asked me to explain the Finder cost structure and you *listened* - really listened, like it mattered, like *I* mattered. I want it so much it scares me. And that's why this hurts. Because I can lose it. I can lose *you*. To the truth. To the record. To the version of you that decides the archive is worth more than the person standing in front of it."

Theo moved then. Two steps. Three. He stopped close enough that she could see the individual lashes framing his eyes, the faint scar on his chin from a childhood bicycle accident he had mentioned once, the pulse jumping in his neck.

"I'm not going anywhere," he said. "Not to the county. Not to a publisher. Not to Drake. Not to any version of the truth that requires me to leave you behind. I - " He hesitated. "I've been building a case in my head for nineteen years. A case against the people who took my grandmother's memory. A case that says: *here is the evidence, here is the timeline, here is the name of the person responsible, here is the proof that they knew and they did it anyway.* And I thought - I *thought* - that if I just built it carefully enough, thoroughly enough, *honestly* enough, it would matter. It would fix something. It would give her back what she lost. But it doesn't work that way. The case doesn't give her back the decade. The record doesn't give Odette back the words. The publication doesn't give Denise back the fifty years. The truth *doesn't fix it*. It just - it just *is*. And the only thing that makes it bearable is the people who stay. The people who *choose* to stay. You. This valley. This kitchen. This terrible coffee."

He reached out, stopped, let his hand fall.

"Can I - " He started again. "Can I stay? Not in the valley. *Here*. With you. In whatever this is. The arguing and the cataloguing and the terrible coffee and the fear. Can I stay in it? Even if you're not sure you can trust me yet. Even if it takes a long time. Even if - "

"Yes," she said.

The word came out faster than she intended. Simpler. Truer.

"Yes," she repeated. "You can stay. I'm not - I'm not ready to say it's fine. It's not fine. It might not be fine for a long time. But I'm not letting go. Not of this. Not of you. Not unless you decide to go."

"I'm not deciding that."

"Good."

They stood in the kitchen, two people who had spent a year learning each other's wounds and a week tearing them open again. The morning light had shifted, warmer now, catching the dust motes in new angles.

"We have a manuscript to finish," Wren said. "The redacted version. The one Yusuf approved. The one we send to the publisher by Friday if we want to beat Drake's release date."

"Three days," Theo said. "We can do three days."

"We can do three days. But - " She met his eyes. "We do it *together*. Every decision. Every redaction. Every footnote. Every comma. You don't open the restricted files without me. You don't photograph anything without me. You don't *decide* what the record says without me. We write it together. Or we don't write it at all."

"Together," he agreed. "Every decision. Every comma."

"And the appendix on methodology - "

"Stays vague on the 1987 discrepancy. Notes that the witness signature chain has irregularities without specifying the nature. References the substation evidence Dev compiled without naming the gap. Protects the people. Protects *you*."

Wren nodded. "Good."

"Good."

He picked up his coffee mug. The coffee was cold. He drank it anyway, making a face.

"Terrible," he said.

"The worst," she agreed. "Mara's recipe. She swears it's an old family secret."

"Your family has terrible taste in coffee."

"My family has excellent taste in *everything else*."

He smiled then. Small. Real. The first real smile she had seen in two weeks.

"Yeah," he said. "They do."

She picked up her own mug, blew on the cold liquid, drank. It tasted like burnt beans and forgiveness and the terrifying, necessary possibility of tomorrow.

"Three days," she said. "Let's get to work."


---

The kitchen table at Reyes Orchard had held maps and ledgers and the scattered pages of Adelaide's correspondence. Now it held the proof copies of the Archive's first volume, each page stiff with fresh ink, each margin measured to the millimeter. Wren ran her thumb along the edge of the title page. *Amity Falls: A Complete Record.* The words sat plain and unadorned, the way Yusuf had insisted they should.

Theo adjusted the lamp over his shoulder. The light caught the silver at his temples, the fatigue etched beside his eyes. He had not slept more than four hours in any night this week. Neither had she.

"Page forty-seven," he said. "The Weathers' notation on the 1998 flood. Mara flagged a discrepancy in the rainfall totals."

Wren turned to the marked page. The numbers were correct. She had checked them herself three days ago, cross-referencing the valley's own gauges against the county records Theo had requested. "It matches. The variance is in the county's reporting, not ours. They rounded down."

"Of course they did." Theo made a note in the margin with a pencil worn to a nub. "I'll add a footnote citing the gauge readings. Source the valley's log. That way no one can say we inflated the protection claim."

"Mara will want to see the footnote before she signs."

"She'll see it tonight. I'm taking the binder to her after supper."

Supper. The word still felt ordinary, domestic, a small rebellion against the weight of what they were doing. Caleb had texted an hour ago. *Mara's making the chili. Come hungry. Bring Theo. We're not letting you work through another meal.*

Wren closed the proof copy. Her fingers brushed the cover stock, thick and acid-free, chosen to last. "Ten days. Yusuf said the printer needs the final files by Friday. That gives us six days to collect every signature."

"Six days for six lineages." Theo capped his pencil. "The Wardens signed yesterday. The Menders this morning. Priya is coordinating the Callers for tomorrow. Dev said the Wicks will be simple - there are only three left in the valley, and two of them are housebound. He's driving to them personally."

"The Finders?"

"That's you."

She exhaled. "That's me. And my mother. And the three others who still answer the name."

"Then it's the Weathers. Yusuf said Old Man Harker is being difficult about the wind records from 2003."

"Harker is always difficult about 2003. He lost a barn roof that year. He blames the bargain for not holding the wind hard enough."

"Does he blame the bargain, or does he blame the Weather who held the wind debt that year?"

Wren paused. The question sat in the space between them, precise and unadorned, the way Theo's questions always did. "Both. He doesn't separate them. The Weather that year was his brother."

Theo nodded. He gathered the proof copies into a stack, squared the edges against the table. "I'll talk to him. Not tonight. Tomorrow, after the Callers sign. He respects procedure. He'll respect a footnote that names the Weather and the debt separately."

"You'll need the Weather's name."

"I have it. Silas Harker. The Weather who held the wind debt in 2003. The role, not the bloodline. The record shows the distinction now."

The name hung between them. Silas Harker. Not Old Man Harker, who was alive and difficult. The distinction had cost them weeks of argument with the printer's legal review, and Wren still woke some nights hearing the echo of Yusuf's voice in the council chamber: *Get it right. Every name. Every role. Every date. If we leave one crack, Drake will drive a wedge through it and call the whole structure rotten.*

Drake. Corwin Drake. The man who had spent nineteen years assembling a partial record of the valley's bargain renewals, who had failed in Millbrook and come here to finish what he started, who was still free, two towns over, while his attorneys prepared motions and the district attorney built a case from the evidence Dev had delivered in a plain manila envelope.

The press breaks and Dev fixes it.

The thought surfaced unbidden, a fragment from the briefing Yusuf had given them three days ago. The printing press - the actual physical press, the Heidelberg cylinder that had served the valley's newsletter and the county's gazette for forty years - had thrown a bearing on Tuesday. The replacement part came from a supplier in Ohio. It would not arrive until Thursday. The printer had called Yusuf in a panic. The Archive's first run was scheduled for Friday morning.

Dev had appeared at the print shop an hour later. He had not been asked. He had simply shown up, as he did, with his toolbag and his quiet certainty and the procedural mind that had once untangled Ambrose Kell's falsified toll records and later identified the exact gap in Drake's account - the missing renewal year that proved Drake's leverage was built on an incomplete timeline.

Wren had not seen the repair. She had been at the schoolhouse with the Finders' verification session, watching her mother sign the lineage attestation with a hand that trembled only slightly. But Theo had been there. Theo had watched Dev dismantle the press's feed mechanism in the dim light of the shop's single bulb, had heard him explain the fault in terms of metal fatigue and lubrication intervals and the torque specification the manufacturer had published in 1978.

"He fixed it in ninety minutes," Theo had told her later, standing in the orchard's twilight while fireflies rose from the grass. "He didn't say a word about why he knew how. He just did it. And when the printer asked what he owed him, Dev said, 'Nothing. The Archive gets printed on time. That's the only payment that matters.'"

Wren had kissed him then, right there among the apple trees, tasting salt and apples and the quiet of a man who had spent his adult life chasing a truth that kept moving, and had finally caught it.

Now, in the kitchen, Theo set the stack of proof copies into the canvas bag he had brought. "I should go. Mara's chili. Harker's wind records. The Callers' session tomorrow. You need anything before I leave?"

She considered. The Finders' verification was complete. Her mother's signature sat on the attestation page, witnessed by Priya. The Wardens and Menders were done. The Callers tomorrow. The Wicks with Dev. The Weathers with Theo. The printer's press running again by Thursday. The final files to the printer by Friday. Ten days. Six days. The countdown had a rhythm now, a cadence that matched the one in her chest.

"Stay for the chili," she said. "Stay for the argument about whether cumin belongs in it. Stay until Caleb makes you taste the pie he's been testing for the harvest festival."

Theo's mouth curved at one corner. The expression had become familiar in the past year - not a smile, exactly, but something close, something that lived in the same territory. "Caleb's pie is a strategic asset. He uses it to extract signatures."

"Does it work?"

"Every time."

"Then stay. We need every signature we can get."

He set the bag down again. "Alright. But I'm taking the Harker file with me. I'll review the 2003 wind data tonight. If there's a footnote to write, I'll write it before breakfast."

"Deal."

They walked out through the side door into the cooling evening. The orchard stretched before them, rows of trees heavy with fruit, the air sweet with ripening apples and the dust of harvest approaching. Somewhere in the house, a pot clattered. Laughter rose - Caleb's, low and warm, and Mara's, brighter, and then a child's voice, high and insistent, demanding the first taste.

Wren slowed her steps. Theo matched her pace without being asked. He did that. He had always done that, from the first day he had walked into the valley with his county credentials and his grandmother's missing decade and his careful, patient questions. He moved at her speed. He waited for her answers. He never filled the silence unless she left space for it.

"Your mother," he said quietly. "She signed today."

"She did."

"Was it - " He stopped himself. Rephrased. "How was it for her?"

Wren watched a moth circle the porch light above them. "She didn't say much. She never does, not about the gift. But after Priya witnessed the attestation, she pressed the pen into my palm. Closed my fingers around it. She said, 'You're doing what I couldn't. Don't stop.'"

Theo didn't offer sympathy. He didn't say *I'm sorry* or *That must have been hard.* He simply nodded, once, and let the words sit where they landed. "She's proud of you."

"She's terrified for me. There's a difference."

"Can it be both?"

She looked at him. The porch light caught his face - the line of his jaw, the shadows under his eyes, the faint scar at his eyebrow from a childhood fall he had once described in the same flat tone he used for archival citations. *I climbed the wrong tree. The branch held. The ground didn't.*

"It can," she said. "It usually is."

They climbed the porch steps together. The screen door slammed behind them - Caleb, emerging with a wooden spoon in hand, flour dusting his forearm, grinning like a man who had reclaimed his own life and would not apologize for enjoying it.

"Late again," Caleb said. "The chili's been ready twenty minutes. Mara's threatening to serve it without you."

"Worth the wait," Theo said. He accepted the spoon Caleb thrust at him, stirred the pot with the gravity of a man performing a ritual. "Needs more cumin."

"Traitor," Mara called from the table. She had a proof copy open beside her plate, a red pen moving through the margins. "He's on our side. Right, Wren?"

Wren took her seat. The steam rose, fragrant and thick. The proof copies spread across the table like a hand of cards played face up. The valley's history, documented and verified and ready to meet the world.

"Always on your side," she said. "Even when you're wrong about cumin."

Mara laughed. The sound filled the kitchen, warm and unreserved, and Wren felt something settle in her chest - not resolution, not yet, but the shape of it forming. The Archive would publish. The truth would stand in the open air. Drake's partial record would collapse against the weight of the complete one. The valley would survive its own exposure.

Ten days. Six days. The countdown continued.

But tonight, there was chili. And pie. And the sound of a family - chosen, earned, documented - arguing about spices while the future waited in the pages beside their plates.

Theo passed her a bowl. Their fingers brushed. The contact was brief, deliberate, charged with the quiet current that had been building since the first kiss in the orchard, interrupted by the growing Drake threat, deepened by the rupture that had forced them to name their fears aloud, sealed by her final choice to tell the truth herself with him beside her.

She took the bowl. "Thank you."

"Anytime."

The word hung between them, a promise wrapped in an ordinary exchange. *Anytime.* As long as there was time. As long as the Archive held. As long as the valley stood.

Wren lifted her spoon. The chili was perfect. The cumin was exactly right.

Outside, the press in the print shop turned its heavy cylinders, devouring paper and ink, giving back the valley's story whole for the first time in generations. Dev had seen to it. The bearing held. The run would meet its deadline.

Ten days.

She ate, and listened, and let the ordinary moment carry her forward.


---

The certified letter arrived on a Tuesday, slipped under the Archive door while Wren cross-referenced Adelaide's correspondence against the town clerk's death registers. She knew the law firm's letterhead before she broke the seal. Millbrook County. The same jurisdiction where Theo had built his career chasing ghosts in the record room.

She read it twice. Then she walked to the front desk where Theo photographed a brittle ledger page, his camera mounted on a copy stand, and set the paper beside his elbow without a word.

Theo didn't look up right away. He adjusted the lighting, snapped the shutter, checked the display. Only then did he pick up the letter, unfold it, and read. His expression barely shifted - a tightening at the jaw, a slight narrowing of eyes that meant he was filing something away for later.

"Injunction," he said. "Filed in Millbrook Superior. Drake's counsel claims proprietary ownership of the compiled research materials - his phrase - and seeks to enjoin us from publishing any derivative work."

"Derivative work." Wren's voice came out flatter than she intended. "He's calling the valley's history his intellectual property."

"He's calling *his version* of it his intellectual property. The compilation, the analysis, the narrative structure. He's not wrong that he assembled it. He's wrong about what that gives him the right to do with it."

"And the judge?"

"Judge Harriet Ellery. Appointed twelve years ago. Reputation for being thorough, skeptical of sweeping claims, allergic to procedural gamesmanship." Theo refolded the letter, slid it back into the envelope. "She'll want to see the actual materials. Both sets. She'll want to understand what constitutes the valley's record versus what constitutes Drake's commentary on it."

"Which means we have to show her ours."

"Which means we have to show her ours." Theo met her eyes. "All of it. The unredacted bargain terms. Adelaide's letters. Whitlock's letter. The ring ritual documentation. The toll redistribution records. Everything we've spent a year deciding how much of the world gets to know."

Wren felt the familiar squeeze in her chest - the old reflex to protect, to withhold, to say *not yet* and *not like this* and *not to them*. A year ago she would have fought this disclosure with everything she had. Six months ago she would have negotiated conditions. Now she only nodded.

"When?"

"First hearing is three weeks out. Ellery set a briefing schedule that gives us ten days to respond. Discovery is expedited; she's not letting this drag." Theo's thumb traced the envelope's creased edge. "She also ordered both parties to produce witness lists by Friday."

"Witnesses."

"People who can speak to the provenance of the materials. People who were there." He didn't say *you*. He didn't need to.

Wren looked toward the reading room, where morning light fell across tables where they'd spent months building something that resembled truth. Mara's engineering reports on the flood mitigation structures. Caleb's orchard yield records, annotated with gaps where his memory had failed. Priya's deposition transcripts from the Kell investigation. Dev's evidence logs from the substation work that had broken Drake's hold the first time. Odette's careful notation of what she'd lost and what she'd kept. Denise's testimony, given freely after fifty years of silence. Yusuf's council minutes. Walt's photographed notebook pages, the ordinary man's extraordinary witness.

All of it real. All of it theirs. And all of it now subject to a judge's ruling in a courtroom thirty miles from the valley's edge.

"We'll need Dev," Wren said.

Theo's nod was slight. "I already called him. He's coming up from the substation tomorrow. Priya's driving him."

"Good." She exhaled. "Good. Because if Drake's lawyers try to paint his evidence as coerced or fabricated - "

"They won't. Not with the chain of custody Dev maintained. Not with the timestamps. Not with the substation's own automated logs corroborating every access point." Theo's voice took on the calm it held when he was certain of his ground. "Dev's work is the cleanest evidence I've seen in twelve years of records investigation. Drake's counsel knows it. They're not challenging the evidence. They're challenging the *standing*."

"Standing."

"Who owns the story. Who has the right to tell it. Whether a community's oral and documentary history constitutes a protectable work or a public domain of shared memory." Theo set the envelope down. "That's the battle they've chosen. And it's the one we have to win."

---

The drive to Millbrook took two hours through country roads winding between ridges dusted with early frost. Wren drove. Theo rode passenger with a binder on his lap, turning pages with the deliberate care of someone who understood that paper remembers every hand that touches it.

"He's not a ritualist," Theo said without looking up. "That's what makes him dangerous in a different way. Kell believed he was serving the bargain by controlling it. Drake believes the bargain is a contract with terms that can be litigated, assigned, monetized. He doesn't want to end it. He wants to *administer* it."

"Can he?"

"Not legally. Not without the valley's consent. But if he establishes that his record is the *authoritative* record - if a court agrees his compilation constitutes the definitive history - then he controls the narrative. He controls what the world believes about Amity Falls. He controls what future researchers find. He controls whether the next generation of valley children grow up knowing the truth or knowing his version of it."

Wren's hands tightened on the wheel. "And if we lose?"

"Then we publish anyway. We just publish *second*. And his version gets the first impression, the citation priority, the media narrative. The valley becomes a footnote in someone else's book."

The phrase landed wrong. Wren glanced over. Theo caught her look and made a small sound, not quite a laugh.

"Poor choice of words. Not a book. A record. A filing. Whatever he calls it."

"What does he call it?"

"*The Amity Falls Archive: A Compiled History of the Valley's Protective Covenant and Its Administrative Terms, 1847–Present*. Subtitle: *With Commentary on the Legal Status of Communal Memory Obligations*." Theo's tone was precisely dry. "He registered the copyright eight months ago. Before we went public with our timeline."

"Eight months. He's been planning this since before the ring ritual."

"Since before we knew he was still watching." Theo closed the binder. "He filed the injunction three days after Yusuf announced the Archive's opening date. He waited until we had a deadline he could threaten."

The courthouse occupied a limestone building on Millbrook's square, its clock tower stopped at 4:17 for as long as anyone could remember. Inside, the courtroom smelled of lemon oil and old wool, benches worn smooth by generations of disputes over property lines, water rights, and the quiet violences that never made the newspapers.

Judge Ellery was a woman in her late sixties, hair pulled back in a severe knot, glasses perched low on her nose. She read the motions without expression, her pen moving occasionally to mark a margin. When she looked up, her gaze swept the room - Drake's two attorneys in charcoal suits, Theo in his navy blazer, Wren in the charcoal coat Mara had insisted she buy for "grown-up occasions," Dev in a button-down that had seen better days but was clean and pressed, Priya beside him in her deputy's uniform.

"Counsel," the judge said. "Mr. Drake's petition asserts a proprietary interest in historical research materials concerning a community that does not appear on standard jurisdictional maps. The respondent - the Amity Falls Archive, represented by Mr. Marsh - contends that the materials in question constitute a communal record that cannot be privately owned. Before we proceed to the merits, I need to understand the threshold issue: does this court have jurisdiction over a community that, for all official purposes, does not exist?"

Drake's lead attorney, a woman named Calder, rose smoothly. "Your Honor, the plaintiff is a resident of Millbrook County. The defendant operates an archive that receives mail at a Millbrook County post office box. The research materials were compiled using Millbrook County public records, among other sources. The injunction seeks to prevent publication within this jurisdiction. Jurisdiction is proper."

"And the community itself? Amity Falls?"

"The community's legal status is not at issue. The plaintiff's property interest in his own work product is."

Judge Ellery's pen stopped. "Mr. Marsh?"

Theo stood. "Your Honor, the plaintiff's work product consists substantially of materials that belong to the community he describes - private correspondence, familial records, ceremonial documentation, and the community's own historical accounting of a bargain that has governed its existence for nearly two centuries. The plaintiff obtained these materials through a combination of public records requests, interviews conducted under false pretenses, and access granted by individuals he misled about his intentions. He then arranged them into a narrative that omits, distorts, and in key instances falsifies the record. He now seeks to use copyright law to prevent the actual custodians of that history from correcting it."

Calder's smile didn't reach her eyes. "Your Honor, the plaintiff's methodology is not on trial. His compilation is. The law protects original selection and arrangement. It does not require the compiler to be virtuous."

"The law also does not protect compilations built on stolen materials," Theo said. "Or materials obtained by fraud. Or materials that constitute the cultural heritage of a living community. Your Honor, we have brought the actual records. We have brought the people who made them. We have brought the evidence that the plaintiff's 'compilation' is not a work of scholarship but a weapon - assembled over nineteen years for the purpose of leverage, now deployed for the purpose of control."

"Nineteen years," Judge Ellery repeated. "Mr. Marsh, your brief references a prior incident in this county. Nineteen years ago."

"Yes, Your Honor. Corwin Drake's first attempt. He posed as an academic researcher. He gained access to the Millbrook County records room. He attempted to reconstruct the valley's bargain terms from fragmented references in deed restrictions, tax anomalies, and vital statistics gaps. He failed. In the process, he caused the removal of approximately ten years of memory from a resident of this county - Harriet Marsh, my grandmother. She is alive. She is here. She cannot tell you where she was for that decade. She cannot tell you what was taken. But the medical records confirm the onset, the pattern, and the fact that no organic cause was ever found."

The courtroom went very still. Wren felt Dev shift beside her, the subtle tension of a man who had once been coerced into redirecting a toll and now sat watching the architect of that coercion face consequences.

"Your Honor," Calder said. "The plaintiff denies any involvement in - "

"Ms. Calder." Judge Ellery's voice was mild. "I have read the police report from nineteen years ago. The investigating officer noted 'unexplained cognitive deficits consistent with targeted neurological intervention.' The case was closed for lack of a suspect. The plaintiff's name appears in the visitor log for the records room on the three days preceding the onset. Is that correct?"

"He was conducting legitimate research."

"Under what affiliation?"

"Independent scholar."

"Funded by whom?"

A pause. "The plaintiff has never disclosed his funding sources."

"Thank you." The judge made a notation. "Mr. Marsh, you mentioned evidence. Mr. Nair - Dev Nair, is it? - your brief identifies you as the custodian of the substation evidence logs."

Dev stood. "Yes, Your Honor."

"Your lineage?"

"Finder, Your Honor."

"And your role in the prior proceedings?"

"I was the one who redirected the toll that took Caleb Reyes's memory. Under coercion from Ambrose Kell. I served my sentence. Since then I've worked the substation - the valley's infrastructure records. The automated systems log every access, every query, every data transfer. Including the plaintiff's."

Calder rose again. "Your Honor, the substation logs are not in evidence. They were not produced in discovery. They are hearsay - "

"They're business records," Dev said. "Automated. Timestamped. Tamper-evident by design. I have the certified printouts. I have the hash verification. I have the chain of custody from the substation's isolated server to this courtroom." He reached into his bag, withdrew a sheaf of papers in a clear sleeve. "May I approach?"

Judge Ellery nodded. Dev crossed the well, handed the sleeve to the bailiff, who delivered it to the bench. The judge put on reading glasses, turned pages. Her eyebrows rose once - a tiny movement, but in the silence it sounded like a gunshot.

"These logs," she said, "show the plaintiff accessing the substation's restricted archive on forty-seven occasions between 2018 and 2023. Using credentials issued to... a Mr. Christopher Vane."

"An alias," Dev said. "The plaintiff used it to gain access to the valley's infrastructure monitoring system. He claimed to be a state inspector conducting a grid resilience audit. The substation's automated verification flagged the credentials as valid - they were forged at a level that bypassed the first two authentication layers. The third layer logged the anomaly but did not block access, because the system was designed to fail open for safety reasons. The plaintiff knew this. He exploited it."

"And the logs show what he accessed?"

"Everything. The bargain renewal records. The toll payment ledgers. The lineage registers. The ring ritual documentation. The memory debt accounting. He downloaded it all. The logs show the exact file paths, the exact timestamps, the exact volume of data exfiltrated. He didn't just compile public records. He stole the valley's private archives."

Calder's face had gone pale. "Your Honor, these logs - their authenticity - "

"Is verified by the substation's hardware security module," Dev said. "Which I also have the certification for. The module signs every log entry with a key that never leaves the device. The plaintiff's access left a cryptographic trail that cannot be forged, altered, or denied. He has the valley's complete administrative record. He knows it. And he knows that if the actual record is published alongside his version, his version collapses."

Judge Ellery set the logs down. She looked at Drake's empty chair - he had not appeared, sending only counsel - then at Calder, then at Theo, then at Wren.

"Mr. Marsh," she said. "Your response brief argues that the plaintiff's compilation is a derivative work of the valley's own records, and that the valley's records constitute a communal work that cannot be copyrighted by any individual. Is that the core of your position?"

"Yes, Your Honor. With one addition. The plaintiff's compilation is not merely derivative. It is *fraudulent*. It presents itself as a complete history while systematically omitting the evidence that contradicts his narrative - specifically, the evidence that the bargain's terms are administrative, not mystical; that the debt mechanism is measurable, not arbitrary; and that the community has, on multiple occasions, successfully challenged and modified those terms. He omits the ring ritual. He omits Denise's release. He omits the fact that the 'Warden of the first debt' was a role, not a bloodline, and that Ambrose Whitlock held it and tried to end the bargain and failed and left a warning that his family buried. He omits the truth so that he can sell a lie."

"And the lie is?"

"That the valley is cursed. That its people are trapped. That the bargain is absolute and unchangeable and that the only way out is through him." Theo's voice carried to the back of the room. "He's not selling history. He's selling despair. And he's using this court to make sure despair is the only story the world ever hears."

Judge Ellery was quiet for a long moment. She removed her glasses, rubbed the bridge of her nose, replaced them.

"Ms. Calder," she said. "Your client seeks an injunction to prevent publication of a competing history. He claims copyright in his compilation. The defendant argues that the compilation is built on stolen materials, obtained by fraud, and that the underlying materials constitute a communal record that cannot be privately owned. The defendant has produced evidence - admittedly, evidence I have not yet ruled admissible - that the plaintiff accessed the valley's private archives through forged credentials and exfiltrated the complete record."

"Your Honor, the admissibility of those logs - "

"Is a question for trial. But for the purpose of a preliminary injunction, I must weigh the likelihood of success on the merits. And the logs raise... significant questions." She looked at Theo. "Mr. Marsh, you are asking this court to deny an injunction and effectively permit publication of materials that your client acknowledges contain sensitive personal information about living individuals. Memory debts. Family histories. The identities of lineage holders. How do you propose to protect those interests?"

Theo didn't hesitate. "We've already done it. The Archive's publication plan - filed as Exhibit C - redacts all living individuals' lineage status unless they have consented in writing.
